    "originalFile": "% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)\n\nAt each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \\subseteq V$ and $S(t) \\subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.\nThe final mask is their conjunction:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:composition}\n  M_{\\text{final}}(t) = M_{\\text{grammar}}(t) \\;\\wedge\\; M_{\\text{semantic}}(t),\n\\end{equation}\n%\nso a token is allowed only if it satisfies \\emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.\nThe conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$. This reflects the enabling dependency (\\S\\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\\mathcal{R}$, a property guaranteed only by the grammar layer.\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\\\"{\\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.\nWe enforce a \\emph{grammar-first fallback}: if no semantically valid token survives grammar filtering, semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.\nStructural integrity is a hard constraint; a type violation is a soft error detectable by downstream validation.\nAmong type violations, DR and CF differ in severity: a DR violation renders the triple ontologically invalid (the entity type lies outside the relation's domain or range), whereas a CF violation means each triple is individually schema-valid but the same entity receives different type assignments across triples.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}\nRecall the tightness of relation $r$ (\\S\\ref{sec:bg:ontology}):\\footnote{For symmetric relations, $|\\sigma(r)|$ counts ordered pairs; the normalization $|\\mathcal{E}|^2$ remains appropriate since we evaluate on directed triples.}\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:tightness}\n  \\kappa(r) = 1 - \\frac{|\\sigma(r)|}{|\\mathcal{E}|^2},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nThe mean tightness $\\bar{\\kappa} = \\frac{1}{|\\mathcal{R}|}\\sum_{r} \\kappa(r)$ characterizes schema restrictiveness.\nTighter constraints eliminate more grammar-valid tokens at type-bearing positions; fallback frequency and residual DR increase accordingly.\nThis dual-factor pattern (tightness $\\times$ baseline violation rate; $n{=}4$ model--dataset pairs, descriptive) aligns with the cross-dataset results: SciERC ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) achieves 94--99\\% DR reduction vs.\\ DocRED ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$) at 45--90\\%; the full range across three models is 42--99\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}; Appendix~\\ref{app:dual-factor}).\nThe pattern extends to WikiEvents EE ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.82$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wikievents-kappa}).\nHowever, $\\kappa$ is necessary but not sufficient for high violation rates: per-relation analysis shows that high-$\\kappa$ relations with unambiguous entity types (\\textsc{place\\_of\\_birth}, $\\kappa{=}0.92$, DR${=}$0\\%) have zero violations, while relations with comparable $\\kappa$ but confusable types (\\textsc{basin\\_country}, $\\kappa{=}0.97$, DR${=}$74.6\\%) are highly vulnerable (Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa-case-study}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-Rectangularity and Sequential Masking.}\n\\label{sec:rectangularity}\n% Data source: artifacts/rebuttal/w2_rectangularity_report.md\nSequential masking is necessary because ontology type signatures are non-rectangular (0/7 SciERC, 23/96 DocRED relations satisfy $\\sigma(r) = \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\times \\operatorname{range}(r)$); independent enforcement would admit 33--37\\% spurious pairs.\n\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\nFine-tuning narrows the fallback regime: DR drops to $\\leq$\\,0.20\\% and CF to $\\leq$\\,1.18\\%, with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp;     "newString": "\\subsection{Extension to Event Extraction}",
    "originalFile": "% Section 5 — Event Extraction on WikiEvents (compact, inline)\n% exp_ids: ee_qwen_wikievents_unconstrained, ee_llama_wikievents_unconstrained,\n%          ee_mistral_wikievents_unconstrained, ee_qwen_wikievents_constrained,\n%          ee_llama_wikievents_constrained, ee_mistral_wikievents_constrained_v2\n% v3 final data confirmed by Director 2026-05-18.\n\n\\subsection{Preliminary Extension: Event Extraction}\n\\label{sec:wikievents}\n\nWe apply OntoCoDec to event extraction (EE) on WikiEvents~\\cite{li2021document} (56 event types, 80 roles, 27 entity types, 245 constraints; Appendix~\\ref{app:wikievents}) without modification.\nViolations extend to EV (event vocabulary), RoV (role vocabulary), and AC (argument constraint, analogous to DR).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: WikiEvents three-model comparison (v3 final)\n%  exp_ids: ee_{qwen,llama,mistral}_wikievents_{unconstrained,grammar_only,constrained_v3}\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{2.8pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}ll rrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-6} \\cmidrule(l){7-8}\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & EV\\,$\\downarrow$ & RoV\\,$\\downarrow$ & AC & CF\n  & Trig.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & Unconstr.\\ & 24.0 & 74.9 & 0.3 & 0 & 11.8 & 0 \\\\\n  & Grammar    & \\textbf{0} & 76.1 & 8.2 & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n  & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0} & 2.1 & 21.1 & 13.4 & 14.1 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & Unconstr.\\ & 10.5 & 88.8 & 0.1 & 0 & 5.7 & 0 \\\\\n  & Grammar    & \\textbf{0} & 77.0 & 8.8 & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n  & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0} & 3.8 & 17.2 & 7.5 & 17.6 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Mistral\\\\7B}}\n  & Unconstr.\\ & 37.3 & 63.9 & 0 & 0 & 2.4 & 0 \\\\\n  & Grammar    & \\textbf{0} & 64.0 & 13.8 & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n  & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0} & 1.3 & 14.9 & 3.9 & 11.7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{WikiEvents EE results (246 documents, zero-shot).\nZero-shot evaluation uses the full 246-document WikiEvents corpus; fine-tuning uses the standard 194/20/20 split~\\cite{li2021document}.\nViolation rates over parsed documents.\n---: not applicable (grammar-only has no semantic-layer metrics).\nF1 $\\times$ 100.}\n\\label{tab:wikievents}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}\nUnconstrained EE produces extreme vocabulary violations (EV+RoV $>$85\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:wikievents}), with entity F1\\,=\\,0 because the parser cannot process off-schema output.\nFull S1 eliminates EV and RoV across all models, which validates the tokenizer-agnostic design across byte-level BPE and SentencePiece.\nGrammar-only eliminates EV but leaves RoV at 64--77\\% and surfaces latent AC (8--14\\%), consistent with DocRED's pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:docred-results}); full S1 drives RoV to 0\\%.\nResidual AC (1.3--3.8\\%) traces to semantically similar types (e.g., SID vs.\\ SEN).\nCF rises to 15--21\\%, exceeding RE levels (10--12\\%) because EE's multi-argument structures increase entity reuse across slots.\nPost-hoc filtering is impractical ($<$0.5\\% arguments retained).\nUnlike RE, EE shows measurable F1 gains: entity F1 0\\%\\,$\\to$\\,12--18\\%, trigger F1 $+$1.5--1.8\\,pp.\n\n% exp_ids: p1_ee_ft_qwen_wikievents_r5, p1_ee_ft_llama_wikievents_r5\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning (preliminary).}\nWikiEvents uses a 194/20/20 train/dev/test split~\\cite{li2021document}; the 20-document test set is a dataset constraint, not a design choice.\nLoRA + S1 eliminates RoV (Qwen 38.31\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, Llama 8.8\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; bootstrap 95\\% CI [0\\%,\\,0\\%] and [0\\%,\\,1.73\\%]) and reduces AC to $\\leq$0.8\\%, with argument F1 within $\\pm$1.1\\,pp; trigger F1 shows larger shifts ($-$5.4/$-$0.6\\,pp) with wide confidence intervals at this sample size.\nCF remains low under fine-tuning (0.77--1.18\\%), consistent with RE composability results (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}, Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nConstrained decoding does not further reduce EE CF because the semantic layer enforces per-event role-type compatibility but not cross-event entity type consensus, the same architectural limitation identified in RE (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}).\n",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section7_conclusion_compressed.tex",
  "old_string": "OntoCoDec formally compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\%, CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC (1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% for calibrated models.\nConstraint tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) characterizes the dataset-level constraint regime, including residual violation rates and post-hoc resolution feasibility (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}); entity-level type consensus and GoLLIE-style encoding are future work.",
  "new_string": "OntoCoDec formally compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\%, CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC (1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% for calibrated models.\nConstraint tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) characterizes the dataset-level constraint regime; per-relation analysis reveals entity type confusability as the critical co-factor (Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa-case-study}), and post-hoc type resolution complements S1 on low-$\\bar{\\kappa}$ schemas (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\nThe decidability taxonomy and $\\bar{\\kappa}$ diagnostic together provide a principled workflow for deploying type-safe IE: classify constraints, compile enforcement layers, and select decoding strategy by schema tightness.\nFuture directions include entity-level type consensus for CF resolution during decoding, streaming inference optimization, and integration with schema-encoding training approaches."
}

    "oldString": "These methods internalize constraints through training; code-generation approaches such as GoLLIE achieve type compliance through format constraints (class names encode types) but with limited zero-shot relation coverage.\nOntoCoDec enforces types at decoding time without retraining while preserving extraction capability.",
    "newString": "These methods internalize constraints through training; code-generation approaches such as GoLLIE achieve type compliance through format constraints (class names encode types) but produce 45\\% empty outputs on zero-shot RE (vs.\\ 0\\% for OntoCoDec), reflecting a coverage--compliance trade-off.\nOntoCoDec enforces types at decoding time without retraining while preserving extraction coverage.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\nWe provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-constrained decoding.}\nXGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\\cite{willard2023outlines}, LMQL~\\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql}, SynCode~\\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and Grammar-Aligned Decoding~\\cite{park2024grammar} guarantee format validity via CFG-based token masking; JSONSchemaBench~\\cite{jsonschemabench2025} benchmarks these systems.\nGCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd} applies grammar constraints to IE tasks.\nIn code generation, PICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard} and Synchromesh~\\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022} enforce type constraints via incremental parsing, and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile full type systems into prefix automata.\nThese methods ensure syntactic or type-level validity but operate on static type declarations; IE requires a dynamic entity registry built during generation, where constraint targets depend on the co-occurring relation type.\nOntoCoDec's grammar layer builds on XGrammar; its semantic layer extends constrained decoding from syntax to ontology-level type enforcement.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}\nGoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie}, GUIDEX~\\cite{guidex2025}, and KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024} encode ontology structure as Python dataclasses and train models to generate typed code;\nREBEL~\\cite{cabot2021rebel}, InstructUIE~\\cite{wang2023instructuie}, DeepStruct~\\cite{wang2022deepstruct}, UIE~\\cite{lu2022uie}, USM~\\cite{lou2023usm}, and UniversalNER~\\cite{zhou2024universalner} unify extraction via structured training, though schema compliance gaps persist~\\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.\nThese methods internalize constraints through training; code-generation approaches such as GoLLIE achieve type compliance through format constraints (class names encode types) but with limited zero-shot relation coverage.\nOntoCoDec enforces types at decoding time without retraining while preserving extraction capability.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}\n\\citet{roth_yih_2004} first formulated hard type constraints for IE via integer linear programming; OntoCoDec achieves analogous enforcement within autoregressive decoding.\nPost-hoc validation~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025,slot2025} detects violations after generation and requires rejection or correction cycles.\nEnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} enforces structural constraints via ASP but requires hand-crafted rules; IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} supports semantic constraints via backtracking but requires manual implementation per constraint.\nBoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025} combines constrained and unconstrained decoding for IE; SchemaRL~\\cite{schema_rl2025} uses reinforcement learning to align generation with schema constraints; DCCD~\\cite{dccd2026} accelerates constrained decoding via draft-conditioned generation.\nRecent work shows constrained decoding can degrade reasoning~\\cite{banerjee2025crane,schall2025hiddencost,formattax2026}; however, extraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\\cite{tam2024letmespeak}, and OntoCoDec constrains only type tokens while leaving entity spans unconstrained.\nOntoCoDec prevents violations during decoding, requires no constraint-specific training, and compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies.\n",

    "newString": "\\textsc{OntoCoDec} eliminates DR violations while maintaining or improving R-F1;",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that formally compiles Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \\emph{grammar layer} (context-free) guarantees JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \\emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) enforces per-triple domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.\nWithout constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \\emph{constraint tightness} ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) rather than schema or model scale.\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nComposed with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), \\textsc{OntoCoDec} eliminates DR violations with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp; beam search ($k{=}4$) resolves the cross-field consistency (CF) trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (R-F1 44--46, 1.54$\\times$ latency).\nUnder zero-shot decoding, DR drops by 76--99\\% for calibrated models.\nThe framework extends to event extraction without modification.\n\\textsc{OntoCoDec} compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and applies to any autoregressive LM across four models (8B--70B) and three tokenizer families.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 2,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 2,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " We present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that formally compiles Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \\emph{grammar layer} (context-free) guarantees JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \\emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) enforces per-triple domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.",

     % Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion
     % Full discussion in Appendix; Mistral boundary condition in app:dual-factor.
     
     OntoCoDec formally compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR\,$\leq$\,0.20\%, CF\,$\leq$\,1.2\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC (1.54$\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\% for calibrated models.
     Constraint tightness ($\bar{\kappa}$) characterizes the dataset-level constraint regime; per-relation analysis reveals entity type confusability as the critical co-factor (Appendix~\ref{app:kappa-case-study}), and post-hoc type resolution complements S1 on low-$\bar{\kappa}$ schemas (Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
     The decidability taxonomy and $\bar{\kappa}$ diagnostic together provide a principled workflow for deploying type-safe IE: classify constraints, compile enforcement layers, and select decoding strategy by schema tightness.
     Future directions include entity-level type consensus for CF resolution during decoding, streaming inference optimization, and integration with schema-encoding training approaches.
     ```

   - **`artifacts/_project/paper/section3_method_draft.tex`** (HEAVILY MODIFIED)
     - Added decidability taxonomy paragraph with 3-level classification
     - Added Proposition 1 (Level 2 ⊄ Level 1) with proof sketch

     - Key addition (Proposition 1):
     ```latex
     \smallskip
     \noindent\textbf{Proposition 1} (Level 2 $\not\subseteq$ Level 1)\textbf{.}
     \textit{Domain/range type constraints for IE with unbounded entity sets are not context-free.}
     
     \smallskip
     \noindent\textit{Proof sketch.}
     Consider $\mathcal{E} = \{\tau_A, \tau_B\}$ and a relation $r$ with $\operatorname{dom}(r) = \{\tau_A\}$.
     An output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types ($a_i \in \{A, B\}$), followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \leq n$.
     Validating the head-type field requires retrieving $\tau_{a_k}$, the type assigned at position $k$ in the entity list.
     A pushdown automaton accesses its stack in LIFO order; retrieving position $k < n$ requires popping all entries above it, destroying information needed for subsequent lookups.
     Since both $n$ and $k$ are independently unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint for all valid outputs.
     OntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups), operating as a deterministic stateful transducer over the token stream.\hfill$\square$
     ```

     ```

     - Current state (after R12 edits, NOT compiled):
     ```latex
     \begin{abstract}
     We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that formally compiles Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) guarantees JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) enforces per-triple domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
     Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \emph{constraint tightness} ($\bar{\kappa}$) rather than schema or model scale.
     Composed with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), \textsc{OntoCoDec} eliminates DR violations while maintaining or improving R-F1; beam search ($k{=}4$) resolves the cross-field consistency (CF) trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (R-F1 44--46, 1.54$\times$ latency).
     Under zero-shot decoding, DR drops by 76--99\% for calibrated models.
     The framework extends to event extraction without modification.
     \textsc{OntoCoDec} compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and applies to any autoregressive LM across four models (8B--70B) and three tokenizer families.
     \end{abstract}
     ```

        "content": "% Section 5 — SciERC Results (Draft)\n% Data source: Phase 2 SciERC registry experiments\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_grammar_only,\n%          phase2_scierc_semantic_only, phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_scierc_full_s2\n% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.\n\n\\subsection{Ablation and Composability with Fine-Tuning}\n\\label{sec:scierc-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} presents the five-way ablation on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, 551 documents); only the active constraint layers differ across rows.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: SciERC five-way ablation\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{6}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-7} \\cmidrule(l){8-9}\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$DR\n  & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF & Tot.\\,$\\downarrow$\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 15.37 & ---\n  & 1.39  & 0.40\n  & 0.08  & 15.45\n  & 8.23  & 31.01 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 14.84 & $\\downarrow$3\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.08  & 14.92\n  & 8.23  & 30.98 \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 11.65 & $\\downarrow$24\\%\n  & 1.51  & 0.40\n  & 2.71  & 14.36\n  & 8.15  & 31.01 \\\\\nFull (S1)\n  & \\textbf{0.93} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{94\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 9.90  & \\textbf{10.83}\n  & \\textbf{8.36} & 30.98 \\\\\nFull (S2)\n  & 0.97  & $\\downarrow$94\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{9.00}\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{31.90} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 docs, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\n$\\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\\ unconstrained.\nTot.\\ = DR + CF (total type-violation burden).\nBest per column in \\textbf{bold} (CF excluded; Tot.\\ included).\nF1 $\\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations.\nS1 = single-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}), S2 = two-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:s2}).\nGrammar-only represents the ceiling of grammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}: vocabulary membership is enforced but cross-field semantic constraints are not.}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nFull S1 reduces DR from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\% ($\\downarrow$94\\%) while R-F1 is unchanged (8.23\\,$\\to$\\,8.36, $p{=}0.58$).\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\%, reaching 0\\% on 4/7 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nPost-hoc filtering achieves slightly higher F1 via precision gains but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).\nCF rises from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Layer contributions.}\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823\nGrammar-only eliminates RV and ET with no F1 impact while DR remains at 14.84\\%, the ceiling of grammar-based decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}; grammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\\%), so CF originates from the semantic layer.\nSemantic-only reduces DR by only 24\\% and cannot eliminate RV or ET; this is the \\emph{enabling dependency}: adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\\% reduction (11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2.}\nS2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\\% vs.\\ 9.90\\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_qwen_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations that vary by schema tightness (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nOn SciERC ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$), adding S1 eliminates or near-eliminates DR: Qwen 1.95\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, Llama 1.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.09\\%, with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.1\\,pp.\nOn DocRED ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$, 96 relations), S1 reduces DR from 0.76\\% to 0.01\\% (Qwen, $\\downarrow$99\\%) and 0.49\\% to 0.20\\% (Llama, $\\downarrow$59\\%); R-F1 changes are $-$0.30\\,pp and $+$0.51\\,pp.\nThe lower Llama-DocRED reduction (59\\% vs.\\ 92--100\\%) traces to higher tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$), which triggers grammar-first fallback more frequently.\nAcross all four model-dataset settings, DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot SciERC) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); the semantic layer alone accounts for 39--72\\% of DR reduction under fine-tuning (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 is the recommended fast deployment configuration: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\%, CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\% at R-F1 40--46, with $+$10\\% latency overhead (\\S\\ref{sec:runtime}).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) with LoRA eliminates all four violation types on SciERC for both Qwen and Llama (R-F1 44.23, 46.11; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.76 & 0.12 & 40.86 & 82.15 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0.01} & 0.41 & 40.56 & 81.43 & $\\downarrow$99\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & ---  & 43.72 & 83.93 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.20 & 0.26 & 44.23 & 85.23 & $\\downarrow$59\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nDR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\% across both datasets with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nF1\\,$\\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations. ---: not computed.}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n",

    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that formally compiles Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \\emph{grammar layer} (context-free) guarantees JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \\emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) enforces per-triple domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.\nWithout constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \\emph{constraint tightness} ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) rather than schema or model scale.\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nComposed with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), \\textsc{OntoCoDec} eliminates DR violations while maintaining or improving R-F1; beam search ($k{=}4$) resolves the cross-field consistency (CF) trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (R-F1 44--46, 1.54$\\times$ latency).\nUnder zero-shot decoding, DR drops by 76--99\\% for calibrated models.\nThe framework extends to event extraction without modification.\n\\textsc{OntoCoDec} compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and applies to any autoregressive LM across four models (8B--70B) and three tokenizer families.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 10,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 10
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",

    "content": {
      "type": "text",

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\nWe provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-constrained decoding.}\nXGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\\cite{willard2023outlines}, LMQL~\\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql}, SynCode~\\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and Grammar-Aligned Decoding~\\cite{park2024grammar} guarantee format validity via CFG-based token masking; JSONSchemaBench~\\cite{jsonschemabench2025} benchmarks these systems.\nGCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd} applies grammar constraints to IE tasks.\nIn code generation, PICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard} and Synchromesh~\\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022} enforce type constraints via incremental parsing, and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile full type systems into prefix automata.\nThese methods ensure syntactic or type-level validity but operate on static type declarations; IE requires a dynamic entity registry built during generation, where constraint targets depend on the co-occurring relation type.\nOntoCoDec's grammar layer builds on XGrammar; its semantic layer extends constrained decoding from syntax to ontology-level type enforcement.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}\nGoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie}, GUIDEX~\\cite{guidex2025}, and KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024} encode ontology structure as Python dataclasses and train models to generate typed code;\nREBEL~\\cite{cabot2021rebel}, InstructUIE~\\cite{wang2023instructuie}, DeepStruct~\\cite{wang2022deepstruct}, UIE~\\cite{lu2022uie}, USM~\\cite{lou2023usm}, and UniversalNER~\\cite{zhou2024universalner} unify extraction via structured training, though schema compliance gaps persist~\\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.\nThese methods internalize constraints through training; code-generation approaches such as GoLLIE achieve type compliance through format constraints (class names encode types) but produce 45\\% empty outputs on zero-shot RE (vs.\\ 0\\% for OntoCoDec), reflecting a coverage--compliance trade-off.\nOntoCoDec enforces types at decoding time without retraining while preserving extraction coverage.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}\n\\citet{roth_yih_2004} first formulated hard type constraints for IE via integer linear programming; OntoCoDec achieves analogous enforcement within autoregressive decoding.\nPost-hoc validation~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025,slot2025} detects violations after generation and requires rejection or correction cycles.\nEnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} enforces structural constraints via ASP but requires hand-crafted rules; IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} supports semantic constraints via backtracking but requires manual implementation per constraint.\nBoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025} combines constrained and unconstrained decoding for IE; SchemaRL~\\cite{schema_rl2025} uses reinforcement learning to align generation with schema constraints; DCCD~\\cite{dccd2026} accelerates constrained decoding via draft-conditioned generation.\nRecent work shows constrained decoding can degrade reasoning~\\cite{banerjee2025crane,schall2025hiddencost,formattax2026}; however, extraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\\cite{tam2024letmespeak}, and OntoCoDec constrains only type tokens while leaving entity spans unconstrained.\nOntoCoDec prevents violations during decoding, requires no constraint-specific training, and compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies.\n",
        "numLines": 26,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 26
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex"

    "content": {
      "type": "text",

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section7_conclusion_compressed.tex",
        "content": "% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion\n% Full discussion in Appendix; Mistral boundary condition in app:dual-factor.\n\nOntoCoDec formally compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\%, CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC (1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% for calibrated models.\nConstraint tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) characterizes the dataset-level constraint regime; per-relation analysis reveals entity type confusability as the critical co-factor (Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa-case-study}), and post-hoc type resolution complements S1 on low-$\\bar{\\kappa}$ schemas (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\nThe decidability taxonomy and $\\bar{\\kappa}$ diagnostic together provide a principled workflow for deploying type-safe IE: classify constraints, compile enforcement layers, and select decoding strategy by schema tightness.\nFuture directions include entity-level type consensus for CF resolution during decoding, streaming inference optimization, and integration with schema-encoding training approaches.\n",
        "numLines": 8,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 8
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/section7_conclusion_compressed.tex"
  },

{

  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section5_wikievents_draft.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section5_wikievents_draft.tex",
        "content": "% Section 5 — Event Extraction on WikiEvents (compact, inline)\n% exp_ids: ee_qwen_wikievents_unconstrained, ee_llama_wikievents_unconstrained,\n%          ee_mistral_wikievents_unconstrained, ee_qwen_wikievents_constrained,\n%          ee_llama_wikievents_constrained, ee_mistral_wikievents_constrained_v2\n% v3 final data confirmed by Director 2026-05-18.\n\n\\subsection{Extension to Event Extraction}\n\\label{sec:wikievents}\n\nWe apply OntoCoDec to event extraction (EE) on WikiEvents~\\cite{li2021document} (56 event types, 80 roles, 27 entity types, 245 constraints; Appendix~\\ref{app:wikievents}) without modification.\nViolations extend to EV (event vocabulary), RoV (role vocabulary), and AC (argument constraint, analogous to DR).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: WikiEvents three-model comparison (v3 final)\n%  exp_ids: ee_{qwen,llama,mistral}_wikievents_{unconstrained,grammar_only,constrained_v3}\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{2.8pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}ll rrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-6} \\cmidrule(l){7-8}\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & EV\\,$\\downarrow$ & RoV\\,$\\downarrow$ & AC & CF\n  & Trig.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & Unconstr.\\ & 24.0 & 74.9 & 0.3 & 0 & 11.8 & 0 \\\\\n  & Grammar    & \\textbf{0} & 76.1 & 8.2 & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n  & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0} & 2.1 & 21.1 & 13.4 & 14.1 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & Unconstr.\\ & 10.5 & 88.8 & 0.1 & 0 & 5.7 & 0 \\\\\n  & Grammar    & \\textbf{0} & 77.0 & 8.8 & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n  & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0} & 3.8 & 17.2 & 7.5 & 17.6 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Mistral\\\\7B}}\n  & Unconstr.\\ & 37.3 & 63.9 & 0 & 0 & 2.4 & 0 \\\\\n  & Grammar    & \\textbf{0} & 64.0 & 13.8 & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n  & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0} & 1.3 & 14.9 & 3.9 & 11.7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{WikiEvents EE results (246 documents, zero-shot).\nZero-shot evaluation uses the full 246-document WikiEvents corpus; fine-tuning uses the standard 194/20/20 split~\\cite{li2021document}.\nViolation rates over parsed documents.\n---: not applicable (grammar-only has no semantic-layer metrics).\nF1 $\\times$ 100.}\n\\label{tab:wikievents}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}\nUnconstrained EE produces extreme vocabulary violations (EV+RoV $>$85\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:wikievents}), with entity F1\\,=\\,0 because the parser cannot process off-schema output.\nFull S1 eliminates EV and RoV across all models, which validates the tokenizer-agnostic design across byte-level BPE and SentencePiece.\nGrammar-only eliminates EV but leaves RoV at 64--77\\% and surfaces latent AC (8--14\\%), consistent with DocRED's pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:docred-results}); full S1 drives RoV to 0\\%.\nResidual AC (1.3--3.8\\%) traces to semantically similar types (e.g., SID vs.\\ SEN).\nCF rises to 15--21\\%, exceeding RE levels (10--12\\%) because EE's multi-argument structures increase entity reuse across slots.\nPost-hoc filtering is impractical ($<$0.5\\% arguments retained).\nUnlike RE, EE shows measurable F1 gains: entity F1 0\\%\\,$\\to$\\,12--18\\%, trigger F1 $+$1.5--1.8\\,pp.\n\n% exp_ids: p1_ee_ft_qwen_wikievents_r5, p1_ee_ft_llama_wikievents_r5\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning (preliminary).}\nWikiEvents uses a 194/20/20 train/dev/test split~\\cite{li2021document}; the 20-document test set is a dataset constraint, not a design choice.\nLoRA + S1 eliminates RoV (Qwen 38.31\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, Llama 8.8\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; bootstrap 95\\% CI [0\\%,\\,0\\%] and [0\\%,\\,1.73\\%]) and reduces AC to $\\leq$0.8\\%, with argument F1 within $\\pm$1.1\\,pp; trigger F1 shows larger shifts ($-$5.4/$-$0.6\\,pp) with wide confidence intervals at this sample size.\nCF remains low under fine-tuning (0.77--1.18\\%), consistent with RE composability results (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}, Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nConstrained decoding does not further reduce EE CF because the semantic layer enforces per-event role-type compatibility but not cross-event entity type consensus, the same architectural limitation identified in RE (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}).\n",

        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 70
      }

  "old_string": "OntoCoDec formally compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\%, CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC (1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% for calibrated models.\nConstraint tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) characterizes the dataset-level constraint regime; per-relation analysis reveals entity type confusability as the critical co-factor (Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa-case-study}), and post-hoc type resolution complements S1 on low-$\\bar{\\kappa}$ schemas (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\nThe decidability taxonomy and $\\bar{\\kappa}$ diagnostic together provide a principled workflow for deploying type-safe IE: classify constraints, compile enforcement layers, and select decoding strategy by schema tightness.\nFuture directions include entity-level type consensus for CF resolution during decoding, streaming inference optimization, and integration with schema-encoding training approaches.",
  "new_string": "OntoCoDec formally compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\%, CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC (1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% for calibrated models.\nConstraint tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) characterizes the dataset-level constraint regime, with entity type confusability as the critical co-factor (Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa-case-study}); post-hoc type resolution complements S1 on low-$\\bar{\\kappa}$ schemas (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\nThe decidability taxonomy and $\\bar{\\kappa}$ diagnostic provide a principled workflow for type-safe IE deployment; future work includes entity-level type consensus for CF resolution and integration with schema-encoding training."
}

Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). 1	% Section 5 — Event Extraction on WikiEvents (compact, inline)

5	% v3 final data confirmed by Director 2026-05-18.
6	
7	\subsection{Extension to Event Extraction}
8	\label{sec:wikievents}
9	
10	We apply OntoCoDec to event extraction (EE) on WikiEvents~\cite{li2021document} (56 event types, 80 roles, 27 entity types, 245 constraints; Appendix~\ref{app:wikievents}) without modification.
11	Violations extend to EV (event vocabulary), RoV (role vocabulary), and AC (argument constraint, analogous to DR).
12	
13	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
14	%  Table: WikiEvents three-model comparison (v3 final)

17	\begin{table}[t]
18	\centering
19	\small
20	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{2.8pt}

25	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{F1}} \\

47	\caption{WikiEvents EE results (246 documents, zero-shot).
48	Zero-shot evaluation uses the full 246-document WikiEvents corpus; fine-tuning uses the standard 194/20/20 split~\cite{li2021document}.
49	Violation rates over parsed documents.

51	F1 $\times$ 100.}
52	\label{tab:wikievents}

54	
55	\paragraph{Results.}
56	Unconstrained EE produces extreme vocabulary violations (EV+RoV $>$85\%; Table~\ref{tab:wikievents}), with entity F1\,=\,0 because the parser cannot process off-schema output.
57	Full S1 eliminates EV and RoV across all models, which validates the tokenizer-agnostic design across byte-level BPE and SentencePiece.
58	Grammar-only eliminates EV but leaves RoV at 64--77\% and surfaces latent AC (8--14\%), consistent with DocRED's pattern (\S\ref{sec:docred-results}); full S1 drives RoV to 0\%.
59	Residual AC (1.3--3.8\%) traces to semantically similar types (e.g., SID vs.\ SEN).
60	CF rises to 15--21\%, exceeding RE levels (10--12\%) because EE's multi-argument structures increase entity reuse across slots.
61	Post-hoc filtering is impractical ($<$0.5\% arguments retained).
62	Unlike RE, EE shows measurable F1 gains: entity F1 0\%\,$\to$\,12--18\%, trigger F1 $+$1.5--1.8\,pp.
63	
64	% exp_ids: p1_ee_ft_qwen_wikievents_r5, p1_ee_ft_llama_wikievents_r5
65	\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning (preliminary).}
66	WikiEvents uses a 194/20/20 train/dev/test split~\cite{li2021document}; the 20-document test set is a dataset constraint, not a design choice.
67	LoRA + S1 eliminates RoV (Qwen 38.31\%\,$\to$\,0\%, Llama 8.8\%\,$\to$\,0\%; bootstrap 95\% CI [0\%,\,0\%] and [0\%,\,1.73\%]) and reduces AC to $\leq$0.8\%, with argument F1 within $\pm$1.1\,pp; trigger F1 shows larger shifts ($-$5.4/$-$0.6\,pp) with wide confidence intervals at this sample size.
68	CF remains low under fine-tuning (0.77--1.18\%), consistent with RE composability results (\S\ref{sec:composition}, Table~\ref{tab:composability}).
69	Constrained decoding does not further reduce EE CF because the semantic layer enforces per-event role-type compatibility but not cross-event entity type consensus, the same architectural limitation identified in RE (\S\ref{sec:composition}).
70

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QJBzP51f2FE6c7QbStr4Wj`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that formally compiles Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) guarantees JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) enforces per-triple domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
3	Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \emph{constraint tightness} ($\bar{\kappa}$) rather than schema or model scale.
4	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
5	Composed with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), \textsc{OntoCoDec} eliminates DR violations while maintaining or improving R-F1; beam search ($k{=}4$) resolves the cross-field consistency (CF) trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (R-F1 44--46, 1.54$\times$ latency).
6	Under zero-shot decoding, DR drops by 76--99\% for calibrated models.
7	The framework extends to event extraction without modification.
8	\textsc{OntoCoDec} compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and applies to any autoregressive LM across four models (8B--70B) and three tokenizer families.
9	\end{abstract}
10	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_016y71XLywo7ssDjNJksrLLf`</summary>

```text
1	% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Large language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity is guaranteed to carry a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.
7	We call such outputs \emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.
8	Violations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\% of triples on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\% on DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 1--2\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates correlate with \emph{constraint tightness} (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.
9	Type violations are ontologically invalid regardless of downstream application: a triple whose head or tail type lies outside the relation's domain/range specification contradicts the schema definition.
10	At deployment-grade quality (R-F1 40--46\%), where extracted triples feed directly into knowledge graphs, reliable type-safety enforcement is needed.
11	
12	Existing approaches leave a fundamental gap.
13	Schema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \emph{increase} by 5.8\,pp).
14	Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} guarantees syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.
15	Post-hoc filtering achieves higher F1 in most zero-shot settings via precision gains, but at the cost of discarding up to 48\% of predictions including true positives; at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral DocRED 73.7\%), it discards the majority of outputs and becomes impractical.
16	OntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation rather than discarding non-compliant outputs.
17	Training-based approaches~\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} provide only soft guidance that cannot eliminate violations.
18	
19	We propose \textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that formally compiles IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.
20	A decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\S\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \emph{grammar layer} enforces context-free constraints (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \emph{semantic layer} enforces prefix-decidable constraints (pair-based domain/range) through dynamic logit masking over a runtime entity registry, with an \emph{enabling dependency} between layers.
21	OntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from any IE ontology, requires no constraint-specific training, and applies to any autoregressive LM.
22	We evaluate two deployment configurations: LoRA + greedy S1 eliminates DR with R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp and $+$10\% latency; LoRA + beam S1 ($k{=}4$) additionally resolves the CF trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (1.54$\times$ latency).
23	Zero-shot evaluation across four models (8B--70B) confirms DR drops by 76--99\% for calibrated models.
24	
25	Our contributions:
26	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
27	    \item A formal ontology-to-constraint compilation framework: we classify IE type constraints by decidability (context-free, prefix-decidable with state, non-local), yielding a two-layer architecture with pair-based sequential enforcement and formal guarantees at each level (\S\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from any IE ontology.
28	    \item An analytical framework for constraint behavior: constraint tightness ($\bar{\kappa}$) characterizes the dataset-level constraint regime across 103 relations and determines post-hoc resolution feasibility (Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}); four violation categories (DR, RV, ET, CF) decompose the violation landscape.
29	    \item Cross-configuration validation: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR$\to$0\% with R-F1 $\pm$0.6\,pp, CF\,$\leq$\,1.2\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC (1.54$\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\% across 8B--70B models. The framework extends to event extraction (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}).
30	\end{enumerate}
31	
```

Constraints compile automatically from any IE ontology.\n    \\item An analytical framework for constraint behavior: constraint tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) characterizes the dataset-level constraint regime across 103 relations and determines post-hoc resolution feasibility (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}); four violation categories (DR, RV, ET, CF) decompose the violation landscape.\n    \\item Cross-configuration validation: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR$\\to$0\\% with R-F1 $\\pm$0.6\\,pp, CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC (1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% across 8B--70B models. The framework extends to event extraction (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

```text
1	% Section 3 — Method (Draft)
2	% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction
3	% via Compiled Ontology Constraints
4	
5	\section{Method}
6	\label{sec:method}
7	
8	We present OntoCoDec, a framework that compiles IE ontologies into token-level constraints enforced during autoregressive generation.
9	The design is guided by a decidability analysis of IE type constraints.
10	
11	\paragraph{Constraint decidability taxonomy.}
12	\label{sec:decidability}
13	We classify IE type constraints into three levels: (1)~\emph{context-free}: vocabulary membership and JSON structure; (2)~\emph{prefix-decidable with state}: domain/range type validity, determined at each type-bearing position given the decoded relation type and entity registry; (3)~\emph{non-local}: cross-entity type consistency, which depends on the full output.
14	Unlike code generation where types are statically declared, IE constraints fall into level~2: entity types are inferred from text, the registry is built dynamically, and constraint targets depend on the co-occurring relation.
15	OntoCoDec guarantees levels 1--2 (grammar and semantic layers, respectively); level~3 is addressed by beam search or post-hoc resolution (Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
16	
17	\smallskip
18	\noindent\textbf{Proposition 1} (Level 2 $\not\subseteq$ Level 1)\textbf{.}
19	\textit{Domain/range type constraints for IE with unbounded entity sets are not context-free.}
20	
21	\smallskip
22	\noindent\textit{Proof sketch.}
23	Consider $\mathcal{E} = \{\tau_A, \tau_B\}$ and a relation $r$ with $\operatorname{dom}(r) = \{\tau_A\}$.
24	An output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types ($a_i \in \{A, B\}$), followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \leq n$.
25	Validating the head-type field requires retrieving $\tau_{a_k}$, the type assigned at position $k$ in the entity list.
26	A pushdown automaton accesses its stack in LIFO order; retrieving position $k < n$ requires popping all entries above it, destroying information needed for subsequent lookups.
27	Since both $n$ and $k$ are independently unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint for all valid outputs.
28	OntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups), operating as a deterministic stateful transducer over the token stream.\hfill$\square$
29	\smallskip
30	
31	We describe the problem formulation (\S\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\S\ref{sec:strategies}); formal analysis of constraint composition is in Section~\ref{sec:composition}.
32	
33	
34	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
35	\label{sec:formulation}
36	
37	\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}
38	Given an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:
39	%
40	\begin{equation}\label{eq:output-schema}
41	\resizebox{.88\columnwidth}{!}{$\displaystyle
42	  y = \bigl\{\texttt{entities}: [\{n_i, \tau_i\}],\; \texttt{relations}: [\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \tau^h_j, \tau^t_j\}]\bigr\}
43	$}
44	\end{equation}
45	%
46	where each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\tau_i \in \mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \in \mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\tau^h_j, \tau^t_j$.
47	The output JSON Schema (\S\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\to$ tail $\to$ relation $\to$ head\_type $\to$ tail\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation, ensuring the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields and enabling sequential domain/range lookups (\S\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).
48	The top-level ordering (entities before relations) populates the entity registry (\S\ref{sec:entity-registry}) before relation generation; unregistered entities trigger domain-only filtering.
49	
50	\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}
51	Using the ontology $\mathcal{O} = (\mathcal{E}, \mathcal{R}, \sigma)$ defined in \S\ref{sec:background}, we write $\operatorname{dom}(r) = \{\tau^h : \exists \tau^t,\, (\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h) = \{\tau^t : (\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\tau^h$.
52	Our enforcement target is \emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)$ for every triple.
53	The decoder enforces this sequentially: $\tau^h \in \operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\tau^t \in \operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h)$ at tail position, achieving exact pair-based enforcement strictly stronger than independent domain/range checks (which over-permit 33--37\% spurious pairs on SciERC/DocRED; \S\ref{sec:rectangularity}).
54	
55	
56	\subsection{Grammar Layer}
57	\label{sec:grammar}
58	
59	A JSON Schema encoding Eq.~\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \texttt{enum} constraints ($\mathcal{E}$, $\mathcal{R}$) is compiled into a pushdown automaton via XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}.
60	At step $t$, grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \subseteq V$ pass; all others receive logits $-\infty$, enforcing structural validity, vocabulary membership ($r_j \in \mathcal{R}$, $\tau_i \in \mathcal{E}$), but not domain/range constraints (which depend on entity types within each triple).
61	
62	\subsection{Semantic Layer}
63	\label{sec:semantic}
64	
65	The semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.
66	
67	\paragraph{Entity registry.}
68	\label{sec:entity-registry}
69	The semantic layer builds a registry $\mathcal{D}: \text{name} \to \text{type}$ incrementally during entity generation: each completed entry $\{n_i, \tau_i\}$ adds $(n_i, \tau_i)$ to $\mathcal{D}$, which is consulted during relation generation for type lookups.
70	At each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\textsc{HeadType}, \textsc{TailType}).
71	\label{sec:position-detection}
72	
73	\paragraph{Dynamic masking.}
74	\label{sec:dynamic-masking}
75	At type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \subseteq V$.
76	At a \textsc{HeadType} position, if $h_j \in \mathcal{D}$ and $\mathcal{D}[h_j] \in \operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only the registered type; otherwise it falls back to all domain-valid types $\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$.
77	At a \textsc{TailType} position, the mask restricts to $\operatorname{range}(r_j, \tau^h_j)$ conditioned on the already-determined head type, further narrowing to the registered type when $t_j \in \mathcal{D}$.
78	This sequential check achieves exact pair-based enforcement (\S\ref{sec:formulation}), strictly tighter than independent domain/range checking.
79	
80	\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}
81	We use \emph{continuation masking} to handle context-dependent tokenizations and prefix-shared types (e.g., \textsc{Sen}/\textsc{Sid}/\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents): a compile-time prefix map identifies valid continuations for each type name, disambiguating over multiple steps across byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral).
82	
83	\paragraph{Grammar-semantic interaction.}
84	\label{sec:interaction}
85	The final mask is $G(t) \cap S(t)$; when empty, grammar-first fallback applies (\S\ref{sec:composition}), embodying the enabling dependency: semantic constraints reduce DR by only 24\% without grammar-enforced vocabulary.
86	
87	
88	\subsection{Decoding Strategies}
89	\label{sec:strategies}
90	
91	\paragraph{S1: Single-pass decoding.}
92	Both layers are active throughout generation; the entity registry populates during entity decoding and is immediately available for relation-level type checking.
93	
94	\paragraph{S2: Two-pass decoding.}\label{sec:s2}
95	A first pass extracts entities; these compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass.
96	S1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.
97	
```

1	% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)
2	
3	At each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \subseteq V$ and $S(t) \subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.
4	The final mask is their conjunction:
5	%
6	\begin{equation}\label{eq:composition}
7	  M_{\text{final}}(t) = M_{\text{grammar}}(t) \;\wedge\; M_{\text{semantic}}(t),
8	\end{equation}
9	%
10	so a token is allowed only if it satisfies \emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.
11	The conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$. This reflects the enabling dependency (\S\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\mathcal{R}$, a property guaranteed only by the grammar layer.
12	
13	\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}
14	When $S(t) \cap G(t) = \emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\"{\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.
15	We enforce a \emph{grammar-first fallback}: if no semantically valid token survives grammar filtering, semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.
16	Structural integrity is a hard constraint; a type violation is a soft error detectable by downstream validation.
17	Among type violations, DR and CF differ in severity: a DR violation renders the triple ontologically invalid (the entity type lies outside the relation's domain or range), whereas a CF violation means each triple is individually schema-valid but the same entity receives different type assignments across triples.
18	
19	\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}
20	Recall the tightness of relation $r$ (\S\ref{sec:bg:ontology}):\footnote{For symmetric relations, $|\sigma(r)|$ counts ordered pairs; the normalization $|\mathcal{E}|^2$ remains appropriate since we evaluate on directed triples.}
21	%
22	\begin{equation}\label{eq:tightness}
23	  \kappa(r) = 1 - \frac{|\sigma(r)|}{|\mathcal{E}|^2},
24	\end{equation}
25	%
26	The mean tightness $\bar{\kappa} = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|}\sum_{r} \kappa(r)$ characterizes schema restrictiveness.
27	Tighter constraints eliminate more grammar-valid tokens at type-bearing positions; fallback frequency and residual DR increase accordingly.
28	This dual-factor pattern (tightness $\times$ baseline violation rate; $n{=}4$ model--dataset pairs, descriptive) aligns with the cross-dataset results: SciERC ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$) achieves 94--99\% DR reduction vs.\ DocRED ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$) at 45--90\%; the full range across three models is 42--99\% (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}; Appendix~\ref{app:dual-factor}).
29	The pattern extends to WikiEvents EE ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.82$; Appendix~\ref{app:wikievents-kappa}).
30	However, $\kappa$ is necessary but not sufficient for high violation rates: per-relation analysis shows that high-$\kappa$ relations with unambiguous entity types (\textsc{place\_of\_birth}, $\kappa{=}0.92$, DR${=}$0\%) have zero violations, while relations with comparable $\kappa$ but confusable types (\textsc{basin\_country}, $\kappa{=}0.97$, DR${=}$74.6\%) are highly vulnerable (Appendix~\ref{app:kappa-case-study}).
31	$\bar{\kappa}$ provides deployment guidance: schemas with $\bar{\kappa} < 0.5$ achieve near-complete DR elimination under zero-shot S1 (94--99\% reduction); schemas with $\bar{\kappa} > 0.8$ require fine-tuning to narrow the fallback regime (DR\,0.01--0.20\%).
32	
33	\paragraph{Non-Rectangularity and Sequential Masking.}
34	\label{sec:rectangularity}
35	% Data source: artifacts/rebuttal/w2_rectangularity_report.md
36	Sequential masking is necessary because ontology type signatures are non-rectangular (0/7 SciERC, 23/96 DocRED relations satisfy $\sigma(r) = \operatorname{dom}(r) \times \operatorname{range}(r)$); independent enforcement would admit 33--37\% spurious pairs.
37	
38	\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}
39	Fine-tuning narrows the fallback regime: DR drops to $\leq$\,0.20\% and CF to $\leq$\,1.18\%, with R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp; the grammar layer becomes a no-op and the semantic layer alone reduces residual DR by 39--72\% (\S\ref{sec:composability}).
40	
```

    "oldString": "Composed with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), \\textsc{OntoCoDec} eliminates DR violations while maintaining or improving R-F1; beam search ($k{=}4$) resolves the cross-field consistency (CF) trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (R-F1 44--46, 1.54$\\times$ latency).",
    "newString": "Composed with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), \\textsc{OntoCoDec} eliminates DR violations with negligible R-F1 impact ($\\pm$0.6\\,pp); beam search ($k{=}4$) resolves the cross-field consistency (CF) trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (R-F1 44--46, 1.54$\\times$ latency).",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that formally compiles Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \\emph{grammar layer} (context-free) guarantees JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \\emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) enforces per-triple domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.\nWithout constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \\emph{constraint tightness} ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) rather than schema or model scale.\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nComposed with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), \\textsc{OntoCoDec} eliminates DR violations while maintaining or improving R-F1; beam search ($k{=}4$) resolves the cross-field consistency (CF) trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (R-F1 44--46, 1.54$\\times$ latency).\nUnder zero-shot decoding, DR drops by 76--99\\% for calibrated models.\nThe framework extends to event extraction without modification.\n\\textsc{OntoCoDec} compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and applies to any autoregressive LM across four models (8B--70B) and three tokenizer families.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

{

  "promptId": "e4ba104c-8ec2-4ad3-bb0f-19529845a187",

    "oldString": "Training-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} provide only soft guidance that cannot eliminate violations.",
    "newString": "Training-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} provide only soft guidance that cannot guarantee elimination of all violation types.",
    "originalFile": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity is guaranteed to carry a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 1--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates correlate with \\emph{constraint tightness} (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nType violations are ontologically invalid regardless of downstream application: a triple whose head or tail type lies outside the relation's domain/range specification contradicts the schema definition.\nAt deployment-grade quality (R-F1 40--46\\%), where extracted triples feed directly into knowledge graphs, reliable type-safety enforcement is needed.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} guarantees syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering achieves higher F1 in most zero-shot settings via precision gains, but at the cost of discarding up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives; at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral DocRED 73.7\\%), it discards the majority of outputs and becomes impractical.\nOntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation rather than discarding non-compliant outputs.\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} provide only soft guidance that cannot eliminate violations.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that formally compiles IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} enforces context-free constraints (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} enforces prefix-decidable constraints (pair-based domain/range) through dynamic logit masking over a runtime entity registry, with an \\emph{enabling dependency} between layers.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from any IE ontology, requires no constraint-specific training, and applies to any autoregressive LM.\nWe evaluate two deployment configurations: LoRA + greedy S1 eliminates DR with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp and $+$10\\% latency; LoRA + beam S1 ($k{=}4$) additionally resolves the CF trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (1.54$\\times$ latency).\nZero-shot evaluation across four models (8B--70B) confirms DR drops by 76--99\\% for calibrated models.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item A formal ontology-to-constraint compilation framework: we classify IE type constraints by decidability (context-free, prefix-decidable with state, non-local), yielding a two-layer architecture with pair-based sequential enforcement and formal guarantees at each level (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from any IE ontology.\n    \\item An analytical framework for constraint behavior: constraint tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) characterizes the dataset-level constraint regime across 103 relations and determines post-hoc resolution feasibility (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}); four violation categories (DR, RV, ET, CF) decompose the violation landscape.\n    \\item Cross-configuration validation: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR$\\to$0\\% with R-F1 $\\pm$0.6\\,pp, CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC (1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% across 8B--70B models. The framework extends to event extraction (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
