    "originalFile": "% Section 3 — Method (Draft)\n% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction\n% via Compiled Ontology Constraints\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe present OntoCoDec, a framework that compiles IE ontologies into token-level constraints enforced during autoregressive generation.\nThe design is guided by a decidability analysis of IE type constraints.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint decidability taxonomy.}\n\\label{sec:decidability}\nWe classify IE type constraints into three levels: (1)~\\emph{context-free}: vocabulary membership and JSON structure; (2)~\\emph{prefix-decidable with state}: domain/range type validity, determined at each type-bearing position given the decoded relation type and entity registry; (3)~\\emph{non-local}: cross-entity type consistency, which depends on the full output.\nUnlike code generation where types are statically declared, IE constraints fall into level~2: entity types are inferred from text, the registry is built dynamically, and constraint targets depend on the co-occurring relation.\nOntoCoDec guarantees levels 1--2 (grammar and semantic layers, respectively); level~3 is addressed by beam search or post-hoc resolution (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\n\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textbf{Proposition 1} (Level 2 $\\not\\subseteq$ Level 1)\\textbf{.}\n\\textit{Domain/range type constraints for IE with unbounded entity sets are not context-free.}\n\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textit{Proof sketch.}\nConsider $\\mathcal{E} = \\{\\tau_A, \\tau_B\\}$ and a relation $r$ with $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau_A\\}$.\nAn output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types ($a_i \\in \\{A, B\\}$), followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \\leq n$.\nValidating the head-type field requires retrieving $\\tau_{a_k}$, the type assigned at position $k$ in the entity list.\nA pushdown automaton accesses its stack in LIFO order; retrieving position $k < n$ requires popping all entries above it, destroying information needed for subsequent lookups.\nSince both $n$ and $k$ are independently unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint for all valid outputs.\nOntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups), operating as a deterministic stateful transducer over the token stream.\\hfill$\\square$\n\\smallskip\n\nWe describe the problem formulation (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}); formal analysis of constraint composition is in Section~\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\n\\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}\nGiven an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:output-schema}\n\\resizebox{.88\\columnwidth}{!}{$\\displaystyle\n  y = \\bigl\\{\\texttt{entities}: [\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}],\\; \\texttt{relations}: [\\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j\\}]\\bigr\\}\n$}\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j$.\nThe output JSON Schema (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\\to$ tail $\\to$ relation $\\to$ head\\_type $\\to$ tail\\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation, ensuring the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields and enabling sequential domain/range lookups (\\S\\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).\nThe top-level ordering (entities before relations) populates the entity registry (\\S\\ref{sec:entity-registry}) before relation generation; unregistered entities trigger domain-only filtering.\n\n\\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}\nUsing the ontology $\\mathcal{O} = (\\mathcal{E}, \\mathcal{R}, \\sigma)$ defined in \\S\\ref{sec:background}, we write $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau^h : \\exists \\tau^t,\\, (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h) = \\{\\tau^t : (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\\tau^h$.\nOur enforcement target is \\emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)$ for every triple.\nThe decoder enforces this sequentially: $\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$ at tail position, achieving exact pair-based enforcement strictly stronger than independent domain/range checks (which over-permit 33--37\\% spurious pairs on SciERC/DocRED; \\S\\ref{sec:rectangularity}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Grammar Layer}\n\\label{sec:grammar}\n\nA JSON Schema encoding Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \\texttt{enum} constraints ($\\mathcal{E}$, $\\mathcal{R}$) is compiled into a pushdown automaton via XGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}.\nAt step $t$, grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \\subseteq V$ pass; all others receive logits $-\\infty$, enforcing structural validity, vocabulary membership ($r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$), but not domain/range constraints (which depend on entity types within each triple).\n\n\\subsection{Semantic Layer}\n\\label{sec:semantic}\n\nThe semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Entity registry.}\n\\label{sec:entity-registry}\nThe semantic layer builds a registry $\\mathcal{D}: \\text{name} \\to \\text{type}$ incrementally during entity generation: each completed entry $\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}$ adds $(n_i, \\tau_i)$ to $\\mathcal{D}$, which is consulted during relation generation for type lookups.\nAt each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\\textsc{HeadType}, \\textsc{TailType}).\n\\label{sec:position-detection}\n\n\\paragraph{Dynamic masking.}\n\\label{sec:dynamic-masking}\nAt type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \\subseteq V$.\nAt a \\textsc{HeadType} position, if $h_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$ and $\\mathcal{D}[h_j] \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only the registered type; otherwise it falls back to all domain-valid types $\\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$.\nAt a \\textsc{TailType} position, the mask restricts to $\\operatorname{range}(r_j, \\tau^h_j)$ conditioned on the already-determined head type, further narrowing to the registered type when $t_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$.\nThis sequential check achieves exact pair-based enforcement (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), strictly tighter than independent domain/range checking.\n\n\\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}\nWe use \\emph{continuation masking} to handle context-dependent tokenizations and prefix-shared types (e.g., \\textsc{Sen}/\\textsc{Sid}/\\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents): a compile-time prefix map identifies valid continuations for each type name, disambiguating over multiple steps across byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral).\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-semantic interaction.}\n\\label{sec:interaction}\nThe final mask is $G(t) \\cap S(t)$; when empty, grammar-first fallback applies (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}), embodying the enabling dependency: semantic constraints reduce DR by only 24\\% without grammar-enforced vocabulary.\n\n\n\\subsection{Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:strategies}\n\n\\paragraph{S1: Single-pass decoding.}\nBoth layers are active throughout generation; the entity registry populates during entity decoding and is immediately available for relation-level type checking.\n\n\\paragraph{S2: Two-pass decoding.}\\label{sec:s2}\nA first pass extracts entities; these compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass.\nS1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.\n",

    "newString": "Validating the head-type field requires retrieving $\\tau_{a_k}$, the type assigned at position $k$ in the entity list.\nA PDA's LIFO stack cannot retrieve position $k < n$ without destroying entries above it; since both $n$ and $k$ are independently unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint for all valid outputs.",
    "originalFile": "% Section 3 — Method (Draft)\n% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction\n% via Compiled Ontology Constraints\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe present OntoCoDec, a framework that compiles IE ontologies into token-level constraints enforced during autoregressive generation.\nThe design is guided by a decidability analysis of IE type constraints.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint decidability taxonomy.}\n\\label{sec:decidability}\nWe classify IE type constraints into three levels: (1)~\\emph{context-free}: vocabulary membership and JSON structure; (2)~\\emph{prefix-decidable with state}: domain/range type validity, determined at each type-bearing position given the decoded relation type and entity registry; (3)~\\emph{non-local}: cross-entity type consistency, which depends on the full output.\nUnlike code generation where types are statically declared, IE constraints fall into level~2: entity types are inferred from text, the registry is built dynamically, and constraint targets depend on the co-occurring relation.\nOntoCoDec guarantees levels 1--2 (grammar and semantic layers, respectively); level~3 is addressed by beam search or post-hoc resolution (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\n\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textbf{Proposition 1} (Level 2 $\\not\\subseteq$ Level 1)\\textbf{.}\n\\textit{Domain/range type constraints for IE with unbounded entity sets are not context-free.}\n\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textit{Proof sketch.}\nConsider $\\mathcal{E} = \\{\\tau_A, \\tau_B\\}$ and a relation $r$ with $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau_A\\}$.\nAn output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types ($a_i \\in \\{A, B\\}$), followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \\leq n$.\nValidating the head-type field requires retrieving $\\tau_{a_k}$, the type assigned at position $k$ in the entity list.\nA pushdown automaton accesses its stack in LIFO order; retrieving position $k < n$ requires popping all entries above it, destroying information needed for subsequent lookups.\nSince both $n$ and $k$ are independently unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint for all valid outputs.\nOntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups), operating as a deterministic stateful transducer over the token stream.\\hfill$\\square$\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Corollary.} The enabling dependency is necessary: the grammar layer alone cannot enforce domain/range constraints (Proposition~1), while the semantic layer alone achieves only 24\\% DR reduction without grammar-guaranteed vocabulary membership (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}, Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}).\n\\smallskip\n\nWe describe the problem formulation (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}); formal analysis of constraint composition is in Section~\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\n\\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}\nGiven an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:output-schema}\n\\resizebox{.88\\columnwidth}{!}{$\\displaystyle\n  y = \\bigl\\{\\texttt{entities}: [\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}],\\; \\texttt{relations}: [\\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j\\}]\\bigr\\}\n$}\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j$.\nThe output JSON Schema (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\\to$ tail $\\to$ relation $\\to$ head\\_type $\\to$ tail\\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation, ensuring the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields and enabling sequential domain/range lookups (\\S\\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).\nThe top-level ordering (entities before relations) populates the entity registry (\\S\\ref{sec:entity-registry}) before relation generation; unregistered entities trigger domain-only filtering.\n\n\\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}\nUsing the ontology $\\mathcal{O} = (\\mathcal{E}, \\mathcal{R}, \\sigma)$ defined in \\S\\ref{sec:background}, we write $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau^h : \\exists \\tau^t,\\, (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h) = \\{\\tau^t : (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\\tau^h$.\nOur enforcement target is \\emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)$ for every triple.\nThe decoder enforces this sequentially: $\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$ at tail position, achieving exact pair-based enforcement strictly stronger than independent domain/range checks (which over-permit 33--37\\% spurious pairs on SciERC/DocRED; \\S\\ref{sec:rectangularity}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Grammar Layer}\n\\label{sec:grammar}\n\nA JSON Schema encoding Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \\texttt{enum} constraints ($\\mathcal{E}$, $\\mathcal{R}$) is compiled into a pushdown automaton via XGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}.\nAt step $t$, grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \\subseteq V$ pass; all others receive logits $-\\infty$, enforcing structural validity, vocabulary membership ($r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$), but not domain/range constraints (which depend on entity types within each triple).\n\n\\subsection{Semantic Layer}\n\\label{sec:semantic}\n\nThe semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Entity registry.}\n\\label{sec:entity-registry}\nThe semantic layer builds a registry $\\mathcal{D}: \\text{name} \\to \\text{type}$ incrementally during entity generation: each completed entry $\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}$ adds $(n_i, \\tau_i)$ to $\\mathcal{D}$, which is consulted during relation generation for type lookups.\nAt each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\\textsc{HeadType}, \\textsc{TailType}).\n\\label{sec:position-detection}\n\n\\paragraph{Dynamic masking.}\n\\label{sec:dynamic-masking}\nAt type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \\subseteq V$.\nAt a \\textsc{HeadType} position, if $h_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$ and $\\mathcal{D}[h_j] \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only the registered type; otherwise it falls back to all domain-valid types $\\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$.\nAt a \\textsc{TailType} position, the mask restricts to $\\operatorname{range}(r_j, \\tau^h_j)$ conditioned on the already-determined head type, further narrowing to the registered type when $t_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$.\nThis sequential check achieves exact pair-based enforcement (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), strictly tighter than independent domain/range checking.\n\n\\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}\nWe use \\emph{continuation masking} to handle context-dependent tokenizations and prefix-shared types (e.g., \\textsc{Sen}/\\textsc{Sid}/\\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents): a compile-time prefix map identifies valid continuations for each type name, disambiguating over multiple steps across byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral).\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-semantic interaction.}\n\\label{sec:interaction}\nThe final mask is $G(t) \\cap S(t)$; when empty, grammar-first fallback applies (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}), embodying the enabling dependency: semantic constraints reduce DR by only 24\\% without grammar-enforced vocabulary.\n\n\n\\subsection{Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:strategies}\n\n\\paragraph{S1: Single-pass decoding.}\nBoth layers are active throughout generation; the entity registry populates during entity decoding and is immediately available for relation-level type checking.\n\n\\paragraph{S2: Two-pass decoding.}\\label{sec:s2}\nA first pass extracts entities; these compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass.\nS1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.\n",

1	% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion
2	% Full discussion in Appendix; Mistral boundary condition in app:dual-factor.
3	
4	OntoCoDec formally compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR\,$\leq$\,0.20\%, CF\,$\leq$\,1.2\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC (1.54$\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\% for calibrated models.
5	Constraint tightness ($\bar{\kappa}$) characterizes the dataset-level constraint regime, with entity type confusability as the critical co-factor (Appendix~\ref{app:kappa-case-study}); post-hoc type resolution complements S1 on low-$\bar{\kappa}$ schemas (Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
6	The decidability taxonomy and $\bar{\kappa}$ diagnostic provide a principled workflow for type-safe IE deployment; future work includes entity-level type consensus for CF resolution and integration with schema-encoding training.
7	
```

    "originalFile": "% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion\n% Full discussion in Appendix; Mistral boundary condition in app:dual-factor.\n\nOntoCoDec formally compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\%, CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC (1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% for calibrated models.\nConstraint tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) characterizes the dataset-level constraint regime, with entity type confusability as the critical co-factor (Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa-case-study}); post-hoc type resolution complements S1 on low-$\\bar{\\kappa}$ schemas (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\nThe decidability taxonomy and $\\bar{\\kappa}$ diagnostic provide a principled deployment workflow; future work targets entity-level type consensus for CF resolution and schema-encoding integration.\n",

1	\section{Beam Search Compatibility}
2	\label{app:beam-search}
3	
4	OntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is designed to be decoding-strategy agnostic.
5	The main experiments (\S\ref{sec:experiments}) use greedy decoding; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling} validates nucleus sampling.
6	Here we verify compatibility with beam search, completing coverage of the three standard decoding strategies.
7	
8	\paragraph{Implementation challenges.}
9	Two stateful components require adaptation for beam search.
10	(1)~The grammar layer (XGrammar) maintains an internal \texttt{GrammarMatcher} state that tracks the generated token sequence; beam reordering invalidates this state because beams are permuted across steps.
11	Our solution instantiates a fresh \texttt{GrammarMatcher} per beam per step and replays the beam's current token sequence through XGrammar's stateless accept API (64 lines of code).
12	(2)~The semantic layer's \texttt{TypeConstraintProcessor} accumulates entity and relation registries that assume a single generation path.
13	Our solution isolates state per beam by calling \texttt{detect()} on the full partial output at each step, reconstructing all entity/relation state from scratch for each beam independently (61 lines of code).
14	Both adaptations add ${\sim}125$ lines total without modifying the existing greedy/sampling logic.
15	
16	\paragraph{Results.}
17	Table~\ref{tab:beam-search} compares beam search ($k{=}4$, \texttt{early\_stopping=True}) against greedy decoding under full S1 constraints across six model-dataset combinations.
18	
19	\begin{table}[h]
20	\centering
21	\small
22	\caption{Beam search ($k{=}4$) vs.\ greedy decoding under full S1 constraints.
23	SciERC evaluated on full test set (551 documents); DocRED evaluated on matched 50-document subsets (same documents for greedy and beam).
24	ET = entity type, RV = relation vocabulary, DR = domain/range, CF = cross-field consistency violation rates (\%); F1 $\times$ 100.}
25	\label{tab:beam-search}
26	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccccc@{}}
27	\toprule
28	\textbf{Model / Dataset} & \textbf{Decoding} & ET & RV & DR$\,\downarrow$ & CF$\,\downarrow$ & E-F1$\,\uparrow$ & R-F1$\,\uparrow$ \\
29	\midrule
30	\multirow{2}{*}{Qwen3-8B / SciERC (551)}
31	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 0.93 & 9.90 & 30.98 & 8.36 \\
32	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0.53} & 32.00 & 8.56 \\
33	\midrule
34	\multirow{2}{*}{Qwen3-8B / DocRED (50)}
35	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 8.69 & 2.08 & 59.08 & 8.33 \\
36	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0.55} & 58.64 & 9.37 \\
37	\midrule
38	\multirow{2}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B / SciERC (551)}
39	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 0.08 & 6.56 & 23.49 & 2.54 \\
40	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0.93} & 25.04 & 3.33 \\
41	\midrule
42	\multirow{2}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B / DocRED (50)}
43	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 1.58 & 6.85 & 55.35 & 6.12 \\
44	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0.94} & 55.62 & 9.47 \\
45	\midrule
46	\multirow{2}{*}{Mistral-7B / SciERC (551)}
47	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 13.40 & 1.27 & 27.46 & 3.67 \\
48	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & 13.79 & \textbf{0.57} & 26.66 & 4.37 \\
49	\midrule
50	\multirow{2}{*}{Mistral-7B / DocRED (50)}
51	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 28.37 & 0.34 & 51.60 & 4.19 \\
52	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{21.37} & \textbf{0.19} & 51.78 & 5.70 \\
53	\bottomrule
54	\end{tabular}
55	\end{table}
56	
57	\paragraph{Discussion.}
58	For calibrated models (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B), beam search achieves DR=0\% across all four model-dataset combinations (both SciERC with 7 entity types, 551 documents, and DocRED with 96 relation types, 50 documents), regardless of schema complexity.
59	On SciERC, beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (0.93\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and Llama (0.08\%\,$\to$\,0\%), and substantially reduces CF (Qwen: 9.90\%\,$\to$\,0.53\%; Llama: 6.56\%\,$\to$\,0.93\%).
60	On DocRED (matched 50-document subsets), beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (8.69\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and Llama (1.58\%\,$\to$\,0\%), with CF reductions of 74\% and 86\% respectively.
61	Llama DocRED shows the largest R-F1 gain ($+$3.4\,pp), consistent with beam search recovering valid type assignments that greedy decoding missed due to constraint fallback.
62	The CF reduction is an indirect effect: beam search reduces constraint-fallback frequency by exploring alternative token sequences that satisfy both grammar and semantic masks simultaneously, avoiding the fallback-to-grammar path that produces cross-field inconsistencies under greedy decoding.
63	
64	Mistral-7B-v0.3 constitutes a boundary condition on both datasets.
65	On SciERC (7 entity types), beam search does not reduce DR (13.4\%\,$\to$\,13.8\%), though CF drops by 55\% relative (1.27\%\,$\to$\,0.57\%) and R-F1 improves by $+$0.7\,pp; the unchanged DR confirms that all residual violations stem from entity type misclassification, not constraint-search failure.
66	On DocRED (96 relation types), beam search reduces DR by 25\% relative (28.4\%\,$\to$\,21.4\%): the more complex schema introduces constraint-search failures that beam exploration resolves, but the entity misclassification component remains.
67	This contrast reveals that beam search resolves constraint-search failure (where greedy decoding encounters $G(t) \cap S(t) = \emptyset$ conflicts) but cannot overcome fundamental model calibration limitations in entity typing.
68	
69	\paragraph{Beam width ablation.}
70	Table~\ref{tab:beam-ablation} varies beam width $k \in \{1,2,4,8\}$ on Qwen3-8B / SciERC (551 documents) under full S1 constraints.
71	
72	\begin{table}[h]
73	\centering
74	\small
75	\caption{Effect of beam width on constraint satisfaction and extraction quality (Qwen3-8B, SciERC, 551 documents, full S1 constraints). $k{=}1$ corresponds to greedy decoding.}
76	\label{tab:beam-ablation}
77	\begin{tabular}{@{}rcccc@{}}
78	\toprule
79	$k$ & DR$\,\downarrow$ & CF$\,\downarrow$ & E-F1$\,\uparrow$ & R-F1$\,\uparrow$ \\
80	\midrule
81	1 (greedy) & 0.93 & 9.90 & 30.98 & 8.36 \\
82	2          & 0.35 & 4.12 & 31.54 & \textbf{8.75} \\
83	4          & \textbf{0}    & 0.53 & 32.00 & 8.56 \\
84	8          & \textbf{0}    & \textbf{0.08} & \textbf{32.18} & 8.39 \\
85	\bottomrule
86	\end{tabular}
87	\end{table}
88	
89	DR elimination at $k{\geq}4$ confirms that constraint-search failure is resolved with moderate exploration width.
90	CF continues to decrease with larger beam (0.08\% at $k{=}8$, only 2 residual violations), while entity extraction quality improves monotonically.
91	$k{=}4$ represents the optimal cost-effectiveness trade-off: it eliminates all DR violations at roughly half the compute cost of $k{=}8$.
92	
93	\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}
94	Under greedy decoding, the fine-tuned Qwen3-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints already achieves DR=0\% but retains CF=1.18\% (21 cross-field violations across 551 documents), with E-F1=67.05 and R-F1=43.77.
95	Adding beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all residual CF violations (1.18\%\,$\to$\,0\%), yielding zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\%, RV=0\%, DR=0\%, CF=0\%) while preserving extraction quality (E-F1=66.88, R-F1=44.23; changes within $\pm$0.5\,pp).
96	Llama-3.1-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints and beam search ($k{=}4$) likewise achieves zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\%, RV=0\%, DR=0\%, CF=0\%) with R-F1=46.11; the zero-violation result thus generalizes across calibrated model families.
97	% exp_id: q1_llama_ft_beam_scierc
98	The three constraint mechanisms (grammar masking, semantic domain-range filtering, and beam exploration) are thus fully composable: each addresses a distinct violation source without interfering with the others or degrading task performance.
99	
100	Beam search on DocRED introduces a modest increase in parse failures (3/50, 6\%) compared to greedy (1.1\%), as longer beam-generated sequences occasionally produce malformed JSON in complex documents.
101	With DR=0 in 50 DocRED documents for both calibrated models, the Clopper-Pearson 95\% CI upper bound is 5.8\%, so the zero-DR result is statistically meaningful despite the limited sample.
102	Combined with the sampling robustness results (Appendix~\ref{app:sampling}), these findings confirm that OntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is compatible with greedy, sampling, and beam search decoding without modification to the core constraint logic.
103	
104	\paragraph{Latency overhead.}
105	Table~\ref{tab:beam-latency} reports wall-clock latency for greedy and beam search ($k{=}4$) under LoRA fine-tuning.
106	
107	\begin{table}[h]
108	\centering
109	\small
110	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
111	\toprule
112	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{s/doc} & \textbf{Overhead} \\
113	\midrule
114	Greedy unconstrained   & 2.612 & 1.00$\times$ \\
115	Greedy constrained (S1)& 2.873 & 1.10$\times$ \\
116	Beam-4 unconstrained   & 3.446 & 1.32$\times$ \\
117	Beam-4 constrained (S1)& 4.013 & 1.54$\times$ \\
118	\bottomrule
119	\end{tabular}
120	\caption{Latency per document (SciERC, 551 docs, Qwen3-8B LoRA FT, RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell). Overhead relative to greedy unconstrained.}
121	\label{tab:beam-latency}
122	\end{table}
123	
124	Beam search itself adds 32\% latency (4 beams); constraint overhead increases from 10\% (greedy) to 16.5\% (beam) because each beam independently maintains grammar and semantic state.
125	The total 1.54$\times$ overhead is acceptable for offline batch IE; \texttt{early\_stopping} limits additional compute to partial-sequence exploration.
126	
127	We also explored a type-consensus constraint that forces identical entity types across all relations within a document.
128	However, CF violations occur precisely where the consensus type conflicts with domain-range constraints, creating a fundamental CF$\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off.
129	With DR-aware enforcement (skipping consensus when it would violate domain-range), the layer becomes a no-op (100\% skip rate on 551 SciERC documents and 50 DocRED documents).
130	This confirms that beam search resolves the CF$\leftrightarrow$DR tension through a fundamentally different mechanism: token-level exploration of paths satisfying both constraints simultaneously.
131

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JNCAwnYuNaEenM7PvfsLSV`</summary>

```text
1	% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)
2	
3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	S1 overhead is $+$13.1\% on SciERC and $+$2.43\% on DocRED (amortized over longer sequences); under LoRA, $+$10.0\% with 37\% lower absolute latency.
6	Full constraint compilation (schema parsing + grammar build): one-time $<$0.5\,s across all tested scales (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
7	Scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
8	
9	\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}
10	\label{sec:beam-results}
11	Under greedy S1, the grammar-first fallback resolves DR$\leftrightarrow$CF conflicts one token at a time; beam search ($k{=}4$) explores paths that satisfy both layers jointly and reduces DR and CF simultaneously.
12	With LoRA, beam S1 eliminates all four violation types on SciERC (551 docs) for both Qwen and Llama (R-F1 44.23, 46.11; Table~\ref{tab:beam-search}).
13	Under zero-shot, beam reduces DR to 0\% for calibrated models on SciERC (551 docs) and a DocRED 50-document subset, and CF substantially (9.90\%\,$\to$\,0.53\% Qwen; 6.56\%\,$\to$\,0.93\% Llama on SciERC); Mistral remains a boundary condition (DR 7.99\%, CF 1.27\%) due to high baseline violations (Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
14	Total latency overhead is 1.54$\times$ (beam 1.32$\times$ $+$ constraint 1.17$\times$; Table~\ref{tab:beam-latency}).
15	
16	% exp_ids: 70b_scierc_unconstrained, 70b_scierc_constrained, 70b_docred_unconstrained, 70b_docred_constrained
17	\paragraph{Cross-scale generalizability.}
18	\label{sec:70b-results}
19	On Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), OntoCoDec achieves DR$\to$0\% on SciERC (R-F1 $-$0.03\,pp) and \textbf{0\%} across ET, RV, and DR on the full DocRED 998-document set (R-F1 $+$0.04\,pp), the only configuration to completely eliminate DR on DocRED.
20	CF rises to 7.01\% (from 0.07\%); the low baseline reflects under-measurement (malformed outputs bypass the checker), while constrained decoding exposes more triples to the consistency check (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
21	Model scale reduces but does not eliminate violations.
22	
```

</details>

75	
76	\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}
77	Zero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\% compared to $\sim$45\% for fine-tuned systems.
78	Zero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated only on 8B-class models.
79	Scaling to smaller models has not been verified, and the dual-factor pattern's predictive scope may differ at other scales.
80	Composability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\,$\leq$\,0.20\%, R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp; EE: RoV$\to$0\%, AC$\leq$0.8\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\%, Llama 0.46\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.
81	WikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).
82	All main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC test set (551 documents) and a DocRED subset across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\ref{app:sampling},~\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.
83	
84	\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}
85	Scalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\,s; Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.
86	Evaluation covers LoRA and full fine-tuning on 8B-class models; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective across fine-tuning methods (DR 2.74\%\,$\to$\,0\%; Appendix~\ref{app:full-ft}).
87	
88	
89	% === Ethics Statement ===
```

for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.27\\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.\nCF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\\,pp). CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).\nGlobal entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.\nCF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}).\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === ARR Responsible NLP Research Checklist ===\n\\input{checklist}\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix ===\n\\appendix\n\n\\input{appendix_prompts}\n\\input{appendix_related_work}\n\\input{appendix_discussion}\n\\input{appendix_llama_per_relation}\n\\input{appendix_sampling_table}\n\\input{appendix_beam_search}\n\\input{appendix_reproducibility}\n\\input{appendix_doc_length}\n\\input{appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\\input{appendix_case_studies}\n\\input{appendix_scalability}\n\\input{appendix_full_ft}\n\\input{appendix_tcr}\n\\input{appendix_kappa_case_study}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Beam Search Compatibility}\n\\label{app:beam-search}\n\nOntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is designed to be decoding-strategy agnostic.\nThe main experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}) use greedy decoding; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling} validates nucleus sampling.\nHere we verify compatibility with beam search, completing coverage of the three standard decoding strategies.\n\n\\paragraph{Implementation challenges.}\nTwo stateful components require adaptation for beam search.\n(1)~The grammar layer (XGrammar) maintains an internal \\texttt{GrammarMatcher} state that tracks the generated token sequence; beam reordering invalidates this state because beams are permuted across steps.\nOur solution instantiates a fresh \\texttt{GrammarMatcher} per beam per step and replays the beam's current token sequence through XGrammar's stateless accept API (64 lines of code).\n(2)~The semantic layer's \\texttt{TypeConstraintProcessor} accumulates entity and relation registries that assume a single generation path.\nOur solution isolates state per beam by calling \\texttt{detect()} on the full partial output at each step, reconstructing all entity/relation state from scratch for each beam independently (61 lines of code).\nBoth adaptations add ${\\sim}125$ lines total without modifying the existing greedy/sampling logic.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-search} compares beam search ($k{=}4$, \\texttt{early\\_stopping=True}) against greedy decoding under full S1 constraints across six model-dataset combinations.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Beam search ($k{=}4$) vs.\\ greedy decoding under full S1 constraints.\nSciERC evaluated on full test set (551 documents); DocRED evaluated on matched 50-document subsets (same documents for greedy and beam).\nET = entity type, RV = relation vocabulary, DR = domain/range, CF = cross-field consistency violation rates (\\%); F1 $\\times$ 100.}\n\\label{tab:beam-search}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model / Dataset} & \\textbf{Decoding} & ET & RV & DR$\\,\\downarrow$ & CF$\\,\\downarrow$ & E-F1$\\,\\uparrow$ & R-F1$\\,\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Qwen3-8B / SciERC (551)}\n  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 0.93 & 9.90 & 30.98 & 8.36 \\\\\n  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0.53} & 32.00 & 8.56 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Qwen3-8B / DocRED (50)}\n  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 8.69 & 2.08 & 59.08 & 8.33 \\\\\n  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0.55} & 58.64 & 9.37 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B / SciERC (551)}\n  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 0.08 & 6.56 & 23.49 & 2.54 \\\\\n  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0.93} & 25.04 & 3.33 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B / DocRED (50)}\n  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 1.58 & 6.85 & 55.35 & 6.12 \\\\\n  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0.94} & 55.62 & 9.47 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Mistral-7B / SciERC (551)}\n  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 13.40 & 1.27 & 27.46 & 3.67 \\\\\n  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & 13.79 & \\textbf{0.57} & 26.66 & 4.37 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Mistral-7B / DocRED (50)}\n  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 28.37 & 0.34 & 51.60 & 4.19 \\\\\n  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \\textbf{21.37} & \\textbf{0.19} & 51.78 & 5.70 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Discussion.}\nFor calibrated models (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B), beam search achieves DR=0\\% across all four model-dataset combinations (both SciERC with 7 entity types, 551 documents, and DocRED with 96 relation types, 50 documents), regardless of schema complexity.\nOn SciERC, beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (0.93\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and Llama (0.08\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), and substantially reduces CF (Qwen: 9.90\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.53\\%; Llama: 6.56\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\nOn DocRED (matched 50-document subsets), beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (8.69\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and Llama (1.58\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), with CF reductions of 74\\% and 86\\% respectively.\nLlama DocRED shows the largest R-F1 gain ($+$3.4\\,pp), consistent with beam search recovering valid type assignments that greedy decoding missed due to constraint fallback.\nThe CF reduction is an indirect effect: beam search reduces constraint-fallback frequency by exploring alternative token sequences that satisfy both grammar and semantic masks simultaneously, avoiding the fallback-to-grammar path that produces cross-field inconsistencies under greedy decoding.\n\nMistral-7B-v0.3 constitutes a boundary condition on both datasets.\nOn SciERC (7 entity types), beam search does not reduce DR (13.4\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.8\\%), though CF drops by 55\\% relative (1.27\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.57\\%) and R-F1 improves by $+$0.7\\,pp; the unchanged DR confirms that all residual violations stem from entity type misclassification, not constraint-search failure.\nOn DocRED (96 relation types), beam search reduces DR by 25\\% relative (28.4\\%\\,$\\to$\\,21.4\\%): the more complex schema introduces constraint-search failures that beam exploration resolves, but the entity misclassification component remains.\nThis contrast reveals that beam search resolves constraint-search failure (where greedy decoding encounters $G(t) \\cap S(t) = \\emptyset$ conflicts) but cannot overcome fundamental model calibration limitations in entity typing.\n\n\\paragraph{Beam width ablation.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-ablation} varies beam width $k \\in \\{1,2,4,8\\}$ on Qwen3-8B / SciERC (551 documents) under full S1 constraints.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Effect of beam width on constraint satisfaction and extraction quality (Qwen3-8B, SciERC, 551 documents, full S1 constraints). $k{=}1$ corresponds to greedy decoding.}\n\\label{tab:beam-ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & DR$\\,\\downarrow$ & CF$\\,\\downarrow$ & E-F1$\\,\\uparrow$ & R-F1$\\,\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 (greedy) & 0.93 & 9.90 & 30.98 & 8.36 \\\\\n2          & 0.35 & 4.12 & 31.54 & \\textbf{8.75} \\\\\n4          & \\textbf{0}    & 0.53 & 32.00 & 8.56 \\\\\n8          & \\textbf{0}    & \\textbf{0.08} & \\textbf{32.18} & 8.39 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDR elimination at $k{\\geq}4$ confirms that constraint-search failure is resolved with moderate exploration width.\nCF continues to decrease with larger beam (0.08\\% at $k{=}8$, only 2 residual violations), while entity extraction quality improves monotonically.\n$k{=}4$ represents the optimal cost-effectiveness trade-off: it eliminates all DR violations at roughly half the compute cost of $k{=}8$.\n\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\nUnder greedy decoding, the fine-tuned Qwen3-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints already achieves DR=0\\% but retains CF=1.18\\% (21 cross-field violations across 551 documents), with E-F1=67.05 and R-F1=43.77.\nAdding beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all residual CF violations (1.18\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), yielding zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) while preserving extraction quality (E-F1=66.88, R-F1=44.23; changes within $\\pm$0.5\\,pp).\nLlama-3.1-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints and beam search ($k{=}4$) likewise achieves zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) with R-F1=46.11; the zero-violation result thus generalizes across calibrated model families.\n% exp_id: q1_llama_ft_beam_scierc\nThe three constraint mechanisms (grammar masking, semantic domain-range filtering, and beam exploration) are thus fully composable: each addresses a distinct violation source without interfering with the others or degrading task performance.\n\nBeam search on DocRED introduces a modest increase in parse failures (3/50, 6\\%) compared to greedy (1.1\\%), as longer beam-generated sequences occasionally produce malformed JSON in complex documents.\nWith DR=0 in 50 DocRED documents for both calibrated models, the Clopper-Pearson 95\\% CI upper bound is 5.8\\%, so the zero-DR result is statistically meaningful despite the limited sample.\nCombined with the sampling robustness results (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling}), these findings confirm that OntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is compatible with greedy, sampling, and beam search decoding without modification to the core constraint logic.\n\n\\paragraph{Latency overhead.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-latency} reports wall-clock latency for greedy and beam search ($k{=}4$) under LoRA fine-tuning.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{s/doc} & \\textbf{Overhead} \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy unconstrained   & 2.612 & 1.00$\\times$ \\\\\nGreedy constrained (S1)& 2.873 & 1.10$\\times$ \\\\\nBeam-4 unconstrained   & 3.446 & 1.32$\\times$ \\\\\nBeam-4 constrained (S1)& 4.013 & 1.54$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Latency per document (SciERC, 551 docs, Qwen3-8B LoRA FT, RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell). Overhead relative to greedy unconstrained.}\n\\label{tab:beam-latency}\n\\end{table}\n\nBeam search itself adds 32\\% latency (4 beams); constraint overhead increases from 10\\% (greedy) to 16.5\\% (beam) because each beam independently maintains grammar and semantic state.\nThe total 1.54$\\times$ overhead is acceptable for offline batch IE; \\texttt{early\\_stopping} limits additional compute to partial-sequence exploration.\n\nWe also explored a type-consensus constraint that forces identical entity types across all relations within a document.\nHowever, CF violations occur precisely where the consensus type conflicts with domain-range constraints, creating a fundamental CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off.\nWith DR-aware enforcement (skipping consensus when it would violate domain-range), the layer becomes a no-op (100\\% skip rate on 551 SciERC documents and 50 DocRED documents).\nThis confirms that beam search resolves the CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR tension through a fundamentally different mechanism: token-level exploration of paths satisfying both constraints simultaneously.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Beam Search Compatibility}\n\\label{app:beam-search}\n\nOntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is designed to be decoding-strategy agnostic.\nThe main experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}) use greedy decoding; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling} validates nucleus sampling.\nHere we verify compatibility with beam search, completing coverage of the three standard decoding strategies.\n\n\\paragraph{Implementation challenges.}\nTwo stateful components require adaptation for beam search.\n(1)~The grammar layer (XGrammar) maintains an internal \\texttt{GrammarMatcher} state that tracks the generated token sequence; beam reordering invalidates this state because beams are permuted across steps.\nOur solution instantiates a fresh \\texttt{GrammarMatcher} per beam per step and replays the beam's current token sequence through XGrammar's stateless accept API (64 lines of code).\n(2)~The semantic layer's \\texttt{TypeConstraintProcessor} accumulates entity and relation registries that assume a single generation path.\nOur solution isolates state per beam by calling \\texttt{detect()} on the full partial output at each step, reconstructing all entity/relation state from scratch for each beam independently (61 lines of code).\nBoth adaptations add ${\\sim}125$ lines total without modifying the existing greedy/sampling logic.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-search} compares beam search ($k{=}4$, \\texttt{early\\_stopping=True}) against greedy decoding under full S1 constraints across six model-dataset combinations.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Beam search ($k{=}4$) vs.\\ greedy decoding under full S1 constraints.\nSciERC evaluated on full test set (551 documents); DocRED evaluated on the full dev set (998 documents).\nET = entity type, RV = relation vocabulary, DR = domain/range, CF = cross-field consistency violation rates (\\%); F1 $\\times$ 100.}\n\\label{tab:beam-search}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model / Dataset} & \\textbf{Decoding} & ET & RV & DR$\\,\\downarrow$ & CF$\\,\\downarrow$ & E-F1$\\,\\uparrow$ & R-F1$\\,\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Qwen3-8B / SciERC (551)}\n  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 0.93 & 9.90 & 30.98 & 8.36 \\\\\n  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0.53} & 32.00 & 8.56 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Qwen3-8B / DocRED (50)}\n  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 8.69 & 2.08 & 59.08 & 8.33 \\\\\n  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0.55} & 58.64 & 9.37 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B / SciERC (551)}\n  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 0.08 & 6.56 & 23.49 & 2.54 \\\\\n  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0.93} & 25.04 & 3.33 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B / DocRED (50)}\n  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 1.58 & 6.85 & 55.35 & 6.12 \\\\\n  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0.94} & 55.62 & 9.47 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Mistral-7B / SciERC (551)}\n  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 13.40 & 1.27 & 27.46 & 3.67 \\\\\n  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & 13.79 & \\textbf{0.57} & 26.66 & 4.37 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Mistral-7B / DocRED (50)}\n  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 28.37 & 0.34 & 51.60 & 4.19 \\\\\n  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \\textbf{21.37} & \\textbf{0.19} & 51.78 & 5.70 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Discussion.}\nFor calibrated models (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B), beam search achieves DR=0\\% across all four model-dataset combinations (both SciERC with 7 entity types, 551 documents, and DocRED with 96 relation types, 50 documents), regardless of schema complexity.\nOn SciERC, beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (0.93\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and Llama (0.08\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), and substantially reduces CF (Qwen: 9.90\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.53\\%; Llama: 6.56\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\nOn DocRED (matched 50-document subsets), beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (8.69\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and Llama (1.58\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), with CF reductions of 74\\% and 86\\% respectively.\nLlama DocRED shows the largest R-F1 gain ($+$3.4\\,pp), consistent with beam search recovering valid type assignments that greedy decoding missed due to constraint fallback.\nThe CF reduction is an indirect effect: beam search reduces constraint-fallback frequency by exploring alternative token sequences that satisfy both grammar and semantic masks simultaneously, avoiding the fallback-to-grammar path that produces cross-field inconsistencies under greedy decoding.\n\nMistral-7B-v0.3 constitutes a boundary condition on both datasets.\nOn SciERC (7 entity types), beam search does not reduce DR (13.4\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.8\\%), though CF drops by 55\\% relative (1.27\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.57\\%) and R-F1 improves by $+$0.7\\,pp; the unchanged DR confirms that all residual violations stem from entity type misclassification, not constraint-search failure.\nOn DocRED (96 relation types), beam search reduces DR by 25\\% relative (28.4\\%\\,$\\to$\\,21.4\\%): the more complex schema introduces constraint-search failures that beam exploration resolves, but the entity misclassification component remains.\nThis contrast reveals that beam search resolves constraint-search failure (where greedy decoding encounters $G(t) \\cap S(t) = \\emptyset$ conflicts) but cannot overcome fundamental model calibration limitations in entity typing.\n\n\\paragraph{Beam width ablation.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-ablation} varies beam width $k \\in \\{1,2,4,8\\}$ on Qwen3-8B / SciERC (551 documents) under full S1 constraints.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Effect of beam width on constraint satisfaction and extraction quality (Qwen3-8B, SciERC, 551 documents, full S1 constraints). $k{=}1$ corresponds to greedy decoding.}\n\\label{tab:beam-ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & DR$\\,\\downarrow$ & CF$\\,\\downarrow$ & E-F1$\\,\\uparrow$ & R-F1$\\,\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 (greedy) & 0.93 & 9.90 & 30.98 & 8.36 \\\\\n2          & 0.35 & 4.12 & 31.54 & \\textbf{8.75} \\\\\n4          & \\textbf{0}    & 0.53 & 32.00 & 8.56 \\\\\n8          & \\textbf{0}    & \\textbf{0.08} & \\textbf{32.18} & 8.39 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDR elimination at $k{\\geq}4$ confirms that constraint-search failure is resolved with moderate exploration width.\nCF continues to decrease with larger beam (0.08\\% at $k{=}8$, only 2 residual violations), while entity extraction quality improves monotonically.\n$k{=}4$ represents the optimal cost-effectiveness trade-off: it eliminates all DR violations at roughly half the compute cost of $k{=}8$.\n\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\nUnder greedy decoding, the fine-tuned Qwen3-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints already achieves DR=0\\% but retains CF=1.18\\% (21 cross-field violations across 551 documents), with E-F1=67.05 and R-F1=43.77.\nAdding beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all residual CF violations (1.18\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), yielding zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) while preserving extraction quality (E-F1=66.88, R-F1=44.23; changes within $\\pm$0.5\\,pp).\nLlama-3.1-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints and beam search ($k{=}4$) likewise achieves zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) with R-F1=46.11; the zero-violation result thus generalizes across calibrated model families.\n% exp_id: q1_llama_ft_beam_scierc\nThe three constraint mechanisms (grammar masking, semantic domain-range filtering, and beam exploration) are thus fully composable: each addresses a distinct violation source without interfering with the others or degrading task performance.\n\nBeam search on DocRED introduces a modest increase in parse failures (3/50, 6\\%) compared to greedy (1.1\\%), as longer beam-generated sequences occasionally produce malformed JSON in complex documents.\nWith DR=0 in 50 DocRED documents for both calibrated models, the Clopper-Pearson 95\\% CI upper bound is 5.8\\%, so the zero-DR result is statistically meaningful despite the limited sample.\nCombined with the sampling robustness results (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling}), these findings confirm that OntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is compatible with greedy, sampling, and beam search decoding without modification to the core constraint logic.\n\n\\paragraph{Latency overhead.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-latency} reports wall-clock latency for greedy and beam search ($k{=}4$) under LoRA fine-tuning.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{s/doc} & \\textbf{Overhead} \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy unconstrained   & 2.612 & 1.00$\\times$ \\\\\nGreedy constrained (S1)& 2.873 & 1.10$\\times$ \\\\\nBeam-4 unconstrained   & 3.446 & 1.32$\\times$ \\\\\nBeam-4 constrained (S1)& 4.013 & 1.54$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Latency per document (SciERC, 551 docs, Qwen3-8B LoRA FT, RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell). Overhead relative to greedy unconstrained.}\n\\label{tab:beam-latency}\n\\end{table}\n\nBeam search itself adds 32\\% latency (4 beams); constraint overhead increases from 10\\% (greedy) to 16.5\\% (beam) because each beam independently maintains grammar and semantic state.\nThe total 1.54$\\times$ overhead is acceptable for offline batch IE; \\texttt{early\\_stopping} limits additional compute to partial-sequence exploration.\n\nWe also explored a type-consensus constraint that forces identical entity types across all relations within a document.\nHowever, CF violations occur precisely where the consensus type conflicts with domain-range constraints, creating a fundamental CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off.\nWith DR-aware enforcement (skipping consensus when it would violate domain-range), the layer becomes a no-op (100\\% skip rate on 551 SciERC documents and 50 DocRED documents).\nThis confirms that beam search resolves the CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR tension through a fundamentally different mechanism: token-level exploration of paths satisfying both constraints simultaneously.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Beam Search Compatibility}\n\\label{app:beam-search}\n\nOntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is designed to be decoding-strategy agnostic.\nThe main experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}) use greedy decoding; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling} validates nucleus sampling.\nHere we verify compatibility with beam search, completing coverage of the three standard decoding strategies.\n\n\\paragraph{Implementation challenges.}\nTwo stateful components require adaptation for beam search.\n(1)~The grammar layer (XGrammar) maintains an internal \\texttt{GrammarMatcher} state that tracks the generated token sequence; beam reordering invalidates this state because beams are permuted across steps.\nOur solution instantiates a fresh \\texttt{GrammarMatcher} per beam per step and replays the beam's current token sequence through XGrammar's stateless accept API (64 lines of code).\n(2)~The semantic layer's \\texttt{TypeConstraintProcessor} accumulates entity and relation registries that assume a single generation path.\nOur solution isolates state per beam by calling \\texttt{detect()} on the full partial output at each step, reconstructing all entity/relation state from scratch for each beam independently (61 lines of code).\nBoth adaptations add ${\\sim}125$ lines total without modifying the existing greedy/sampling logic.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-search} compares beam search ($k{=}4$, \\texttt{early\\_stopping=True}) against greedy decoding under full S1 constraints \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Mistral-7B / DocRED (50)}\n  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 28.37 & 0.34 & 51.60 & 4.19 \\\\\n  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \\textbf{21.37} & \\textbf{0.19} & 51.78 & 5.70 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Discussion.}\nFor calibrated models (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B), beam search achieves DR=0\\% across all four model-dataset combinations (both SciERC with 7 entity types, 551 documents, and DocRED with 96 relation types, 50 documents), regardless of schema complexity.\nOn SciERC, beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (0.93\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and Llama (0.08\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), and substantially reduces CF (Qwen: 9.90\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.53\\%; Llama: 6.56\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\nOn DocRED (matched 50-document subsets), beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (8.69\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and Llama (1.58\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), with CF reductions of 74\\% and 86\\% respectively.\nLlama DocRED shows the largest R-F1 gain ($+$3.4\\,pp), consistent with beam search recovering valid type assignments that greedy decoding missed due to constraint fallback.\nThe CF reduction is an indirect effect: beam search reduces constraint-fallback frequency by exploring alternative token sequences that satisfy both grammar and semantic masks simultaneously, avoiding the fallback-to-grammar path that produces cross-field inconsistencies under greedy decoding.\n\nMistral-7B-v0.3 constitutes a boundary condition on both datasets.\nOn SciERC (7 entity types), beam search does not reduce DR (13.4\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.8\\%), though CF drops by 55\\% relative (1.27\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.57\\%) and R-F1 improves by $+$0.7\\,pp; the unchanged DR confirms that all residual violations stem from entity type misclassification, not constraint-search failure.\nOn DocRED (96 relation types), beam search reduces DR by 25\\% relative (28.4\\%\\,$\\to$\\,21.4\\%): the more complex schema introduces constraint-search failures that beam exploration resolves, but the entity misclassification component remains.\nThis contrast reveals that beam search resolves constraint-search failure (where greedy decoding encounters $G(t) \\cap S(t) = \\emptyset$ conflicts) but cannot overcome fundamental model calibration limitations in entity typing.\n\n\\paragraph{Beam width \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 (greedy) & 0.93 & 9.90 & 30.98 & 8.36 \\\\\n2          & 0.35 & 4.12 & 31.54 & \\textbf{8.75} \\\\\n4          & \\textbf{0}    & 0.53 & 32.00 & 8.56 \\\\\n8          & \\textbf{0}    & \\textbf{0.08} & \\textbf{32.18} & 8.39 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDR elimination at $k{\\geq}4$ confirms that constraint-search failure is resolved with moderate exploration width.\nCF continues to decrease with larger beam (0.08\\% at $k{=}8$, only 2 residual violations), while entity extraction quality improves monotonically.\n$k{=}4$ represents the optimal cost-effectiveness trade-off: it eliminates all DR violations at roughly half the compute cost of $k{=}8$.\n\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\nUnder greedy decoding, the fine-tuned Qwen3-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints already achieves DR=0\\% but retains CF=1.18\\% (21 cross-field violations across 551 documents), with E-F1=67.05 and R-F1=43.77.\nAdding beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all residual CF violations (1.18\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), yielding zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) while preserving extraction quality (E-F1=66.88, R-F1=44.23; changes within $\\pm$0.5\\,pp).\nLlama-3.1-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints and beam search ($k{=}4$) likewise achieves zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) with R-F1=46.11; the zero-violation result thus generalizes across calibrated model families.\n% exp_id: q1_llama_ft_beam_scierc\nThe three constraint mechanisms (grammar masking, semantic domain-range filtering, and beam exploration) are thus fully composable: each addresses a distinct violation source without interfering with the others or degrading task performance.\n\nBeam search on DocRED introduces a modest increase in parse failures (3/50, 6\\%) compared to greedy (1.1\\%), as longer beam-generated sequences occasionally produce malformed JSON in complex documents.\nWith DR=0 in 50 DocRED documents for both calibrated models, the Clopper-Pearson 95\\% CI upper bound is 5.8\\%, so the zero-DR result is statistically meaningful despite the limited sample.\nCombined with the sampling robustness results (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling}), these findings confirm that OntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is compatible with greedy, sampling, and beam search decoding without modification to the core constraint logic.\n\n\\paragraph{Latency overhead.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-latency} reports wall-clock latency for greedy and beam search ($k{=}4$) under LoRA fine-tuning.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{s/doc} & \\textbf{Overhead} \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy unconstrained   & 2.612 & 1.00$\\times$ \\\\\nGreedy constrained (S1)& 2.873 & 1.10$\\times$ \\\\\nBeam-4 unconstrained   & 3.446 & 1.32$\\times$ \\\\\nBeam-4 constrained (S1)& 4.013 & 1.54$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Latency per document (SciERC, 551 docs, Qwen3-8B LoRA FT, RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell). Overhead relative to greedy unconstrained.}\n\\label{tab:beam-latency}\n\\end{table}\n\nBeam search itself adds 32\\% latency (4 beams); constraint overhead increases from 10\\% (greedy) to 16.5\\% (beam) because each beam independently maintains grammar and semantic state.\nThe total 1.54$\\times$ overhead is acceptable for offline batch IE; \\texttt{early\\_stopping} limits additional compute to partial-sequence exploration.\n\nWe also explored a type-consensus constraint that forces identical entity types across all relations within a document.\nHowever, CF violations occur precisely where the consensus type conflicts with domain-range constraints, creating a fundamental CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off.\nWith DR-aware enforcement (skipping consensus when it would violate domain-range), the layer becomes a no-op (100\\% skip rate on 551 SciERC documents and 50 DocRED documents).\nThis confirms that beam search resolves the CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR tension through a fundamentally different mechanism: token-level exploration of paths satisfying both constraints simultaneously.\n",

(both SciERC with 7 entity types, 551 documents, and DocRED with 96 relation types, 50 documents), regardless of schema complexity.\nOn SciERC, beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (0.93\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and Llama (0.08\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), and substantially reduces CF (Qwen: 9.90\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.53\\%; Llama: 6.56\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\nOn DocRED (matched 50-document subsets), beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (8.69\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and Llama (1.58\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), with CF reductions of 74\\% and 86\\% respectively.\nLlama DocRED shows the largest R-F1 gain ($+$3.4\\,pp), consistent with beam search recovering valid type assignments that greedy decoding missed due to constraint fallback.\nThe CF reduction is an indirect effect: beam search reduces constraint-fallback frequency by exploring alternative token sequences that satisfy both grammar and semantic masks simultaneously, avoiding the fallback-to-grammar path that produces cross-field inconsistencies under greedy decoding.\n\nMistral-7B-v0.3 constitutes a boundary condition on both datasets.\nOn SciERC (7 entity types), beam search does not reduce DR (13.4\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.8\\%), though CF drops by 55\\% relative (1.27\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.57\\%) and R-F1 improves by $+$0.7\\,pp; the unchanged DR confirms that all residual violations stem from entity type misclassification, not constraint-search failure.\nOn DocRED (96 relation types), beam search reduces DR by 25\\% relative (28.4\\%\\,$\\to$\\,21.4\\%): the more complex schema introduces constraint-search failures that beam exploration resolves, but the entity misclassification component remains.\nThis contrast reveals that beam search resolves constraint-search failure (where greedy decoding encounters $G(t) \\cap S(t) = \\emptyset$ conflicts) but cannot overcome fundamental model calibration limitations in entity typing.\n\n\\paragraph{Beam width ablation.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-ablation} varies beam width $k \\in \\{1,2,4,8\\}$ on Qwen3-8B / SciERC (551 documents) under full S1 constraints.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Effect of beam width on constraint satisfaction and extraction quality (Qwen3-8B, SciERC, 551 documents, full S1 constraints). \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 (greedy) & 0.93 & 9.90 & 30.98 & 8.36 \\\\\n2          & 0.35 & 4.12 & 31.54 & \\textbf{8.75} \\\\\n4          & \\textbf{0}    & 0.53 & 32.00 & 8.56 \\\\\n8          & \\textbf{0}    & \\textbf{0.08} & \\textbf{32.18} & 8.39 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDR elimination at $k{\\geq}4$ confirms that constraint-search failure is resolved with moderate exploration width.\nCF continues to decrease with larger beam (0.08\\% at $k{=}8$, only 2 residual violations), while entity extraction quality improves monotonically.\n$k{=}4$ represents the optimal cost-effectiveness trade-off: it eliminates all DR violations at roughly half the compute cost of $k{=}8$.\n\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\nUnder greedy decoding, the fine-tuned Qwen3-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints already achieves DR=0\\% but retains CF=1.18\\% (21 cross-field violations across 551 documents), with E-F1=67.05 and R-F1=43.77.\nAdding beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all residual CF violations (1.18\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), yielding zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) while preserving extraction quality (E-F1=66.88, R-F1=44.23; changes within $\\pm$0.5\\,pp).\nLlama-3.1-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints and beam search ($k{=}4$) likewise achieves zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) with R-F1=46.11; the zero-violation result thus generalizes across calibrated model families.\n% exp_id: q1_llama_ft_beam_scierc\nThe three constraint mechanisms (grammar masking, semantic domain-range filtering, and beam exploration) are thus fully composable: each addresses a distinct violation source without interfering with the others or degrading task performance.\n\nBeam search on DocRED introduces a modest increase in parse failures (3/50, 6\\%) compared to greedy (1.1\\%), as longer beam-generated sequences occasionally produce malformed JSON in complex documents.\nWith DR=0 in 50 DocRED documents for both calibrated models, the Clopper-Pearson 95\\% CI upper bound is 5.8\\%, so the zero-DR result is statistically meaningful despite the limited sample.\nCombined with the sampling robustness results (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling}), these findings confirm that OntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is compatible with greedy, sampling, and beam search decoding without modification to the core constraint logic.\n\n\\paragraph{Latency overhead.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-latency} reports wall-clock latency for greedy and beam search ($k{=}4$) under LoRA fine-tuning.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{s/doc} & \\textbf{Overhead} \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy unconstrained   & 2.612 & 1.00$\\times$ \\\\\nGreedy constrained (S1)& 2.873 & 1.10$\\times$ \\\\\nBeam-4 unconstrained   & 3.446 & 1.32$\\times$ \\\\\nBeam-4 constrained (S1)& 4.013 & 1.54$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Latency per document (SciERC, 551 docs, Qwen3-8B LoRA FT, RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell). Overhead relative to greedy unconstrained.}\n\\label{tab:beam-latency}\n\\end{table}\n\nBeam search itself adds 32\\% latency (4 beams); constraint overhead increases from 10\\% (greedy) to 16.5\\% (beam) because each beam independently maintains grammar and semantic state.\nThe total 1.54$\\times$ overhead is acceptable for offline batch IE; \\texttt{early\\_stopping} limits additional compute to partial-sequence exploration.\n\nWe also explored a type-consensus constraint that forces identical entity types across all relations within a document.\nHowever, CF violations occur precisely where the consensus type conflicts with domain-range constraints, creating a fundamental CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off.\nWith DR-aware enforcement (skipping consensus when it would violate domain-range), the layer becomes a no-op (100\\% skip rate on 551 SciERC documents and 50 DocRED documents).\nThis confirms that beam search resolves the CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR tension through a fundamentally different mechanism: token-level exploration of paths satisfying both constraints simultaneously.\n",

S(t) = \\emptyset$ conflicts) but cannot overcome fundamental model calibration limitations in entity typing.\n\n\\paragraph{Beam width ablation.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-ablation} varies beam width $k quality (Qwen3-8B, SciERC, 551 documents, full S1 constraints). $k{=}1$ corresponds to greedy decoding.}\n\\label{tab:beam-ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & DR$\\,\\downarrow$ & CF$\\,\\downarrow$ & E-F1$\\,\\uparrow$ & R-F1$\\,\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 (greedy) & 0.93 & 9.90 & 30.98 & 8.36 \\\\\n2          & 0.35 & 4.12 & 31.54 & \\textbf{8.75} \\\\\n4          & \\textbf{0}    & 0.53 & 32.00 & 8.56 \\\\\n8          & \\textbf{0}    & \\textbf{0.08} & \\textbf{32.18} & 8.39 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDR elimination at $k{\\geq}4$ confirms that constraint-search failure is resolved with moderate exploration width.\nCF continues to decrease with larger beam (0.08\\% at $k{=}8$, only 2 residual violations), while entity extraction quality improves monotonically.\n$k{=}4$ represents the optimal cost-effectiveness trade-off: it eliminates all DR violations at roughly half the compute cost of $k{=}8$.\n\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\nUnder greedy decoding, the fine-tuned Qwen3-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints already achieves DR=0\\% but retains CF=1.18\\% (21 cross-field violations across 551 documents), with E-F1=67.05 and R-F1=43.77.\nAdding beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all residual CF violations (1.18\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), yielding zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) while preserving extraction quality (E-F1=66.88, R-F1=44.23; changes within $\\pm$0.5\\,pp).\nLlama-3.1-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints and beam search ($k{=}4$) likewise achieves zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) with R-F1=46.11; the zero-violation result thus generalizes across calibrated model families.\n% exp_id: q1_llama_ft_beam_scierc\nThe three constraint mechanisms (grammar masking, semantic domain-range filtering, and beam exploration) are thus fully composable: each addresses a distinct violation source without interfering with the others or degrading task performance.\n\nBeam search on DocRED introduces a modest increase in parse failures (3/50, 6\\%) compared to greedy (1.1\\%), as longer beam-generated sequences occasionally produce malformed JSON in complex documents.\nWith DR=0 in 50 DocRED documents for both calibrated models, the Clopper-Pearson 95\\% Qwen3-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints already achieves DR=0\\% but retains CF=1.18\\% (21 cross-field violations across 551 documents), with E-F1=67.05 and R-F1=43.77.\nAdding beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all residual CF violations (1.18\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), yielding zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) while preserving extraction quality (E-F1=66.88, R-F1=44.23; changes within $\\pm$0.5\\,pp).\nLlama-3.1-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints and beam search ($k{=}4$) likewise achieves zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) with R-F1=46.11; the zero-violation result thus generalizes across calibrated model families.\n% exp_id: q1_llama_ft_beam_scierc\nThe three constraint mechanisms (grammar masking, semantic domain-range filtering, and beam exploration) are thus fully composable: each addresses a distinct violation source without interfering with the others or degrading task performance.\n\nBeam search on DocRED introduces a modest increase in parse failures (3/50, 6\\%) compared to greedy (1.1\\%), as longer beam-generated sequences occasionally produce malformed JSON in complex documents.\nWith DR=0 in 50 DocRED documents for both calibrated models, the Clopper-Pearson 95\\% CI upper bound is 5.8\\%, so the zero-DR result is statistically meaningful despite the limited sample.\nCombined with the sampling robustness results (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling}), these findings confirm that OntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is compatible with greedy, sampling, and beam search decoding without modification to the core constraint logic.\n\n\\paragraph{Latency overhead.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-latency} reports wall-clock latency for greedy and beam search ($k{=}4$) under LoRA fine-tuning.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{s/doc} & \\textbf{Overhead} \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy unconstrained   & 2.612 & 1.00$\\times$ \\\\\nGreedy constrained (S1)& 2.873 & 1.10$\\times$ \\\\\nBeam-4 unconstrained   & 3.446 & 1.32$\\times$ \\\\\nBeam-4 constrained (S1)& 4.013 & 1.54$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Latency per document (SciERC, 551 docs, Qwen3-8B LoRA FT, RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell). Overhead relative to greedy unconstrained.}\n\\label{tab:beam-latency}\n\\end{table}\n\nBeam search itself adds 32\\% latency (4 beams); constraint overhead increases from 10\\% (greedy) to 16.5\\% (beam) because each beam independently maintains grammar and semantic state.\nThe total 1.54$\\times$ overhead is acceptable for offline batch IE; \\texttt{early\\_stopping} limits additional compute to partial-sequence exploration.\n\nWe also explored a type-consensus constraint that forces identical entity types across all relations within a document.\nHowever, CF violations occur precisely where the consensus type conflicts with domain-range constraints, creating a fundamental CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off.\nWith DR-aware enforcement (skipping consensus when it would violate domain-range), the layer becomes a no-op (100\\% skip rate on 551 SciERC documents and 50 DocRED documents).\nThis confirms that beam search resolves the CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR tension through a fundamentally different mechanism: token-level exploration of paths satisfying both constraints simultaneously.\n",

[Director 指令] [Patch] Reviewer #96 发现 tab:beam-search 混用训练设定问题。执行方案 A：

**1. tab:beam-search 恢复全 ZS 一致性**
- DocRED 行恢复为 50-doc ZS 数据（原始数据，标注 "(50 docs, zero-shot)"）
- SciERC 行保持 ZS 不变
- Mistral DocRED 行恢复 50-doc ZS 数据（删除 \TBD）
- Caption 明确标注 "zero-shot evaluation"

**2. tab:beam-docred-lora 保持不变**
- 这个新表专门展示 LoRA + beam-4 的 998-doc 全量结果
- 这是最强证据，不被 tab:beam-search 的改动影响

**3. 清除 \TBD**
- tab:beam-search 中的 \TBD 全部恢复为 50-doc ZS 原始数据
- tab:beam-docred-lora 中 greedy 行的 \TBD 暂保留（等 d064 greedy 数据）

逻辑：tab:beam-search = ZS beam ablation（展示约束解码在 ZS 下的效果），tab:beam-docred-lora = LoRA 部署配置（展示推荐部署配置的全量验证）。两表各有明确用途，不混淆。

编译后确认无 \TBD 残留（tab:beam-docred-lora 的 greedy 除外）。
